Data are based on extrapolation from the literature, expert opinion, and clinical experience. Patients receiving 5 mg/day or less of prednisone should receive their normal daily replacement, but do not require supplementation. Patients who receive greater than 5 mg/day of prednisone should receive the above therapy in addition to their maintenance therapy.

From Coursin DB, Wood KE. Corticosteroid Supplementation for Adrenal Insufficiency. _JAMA_ . 2002;287:236–240.

of at least 750 mcg/day (or beclomethasone of at least 1500 mcg/day) for more than 3 weeks, high potency topical steroids used for greater than 2 g/day for more than 2 weeks, or 3 or more intra- articular or spinal glucocorticoid injections within 3 months before surgery may be at risk of HPA suppression.<sup>96–98</sup> For these patients it is suggested that HPA suppression should be investigated preoperatively. However, this is rarely done in clinical practice and patients are supplemented with steroids based on the presumed surgical stress.

Preoperative preparation should ensure repletion of intravascular volume and correction of electrolyte abnormalities. Patients presenting with an adrenal crisis (e.g., hypotension from distributive shock, abdominal pain, vomiting, hypoglycemia, hyperkalemia) should be treated immediately with glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid replacement.

### Intraoperative

Intraoperative monitoring (e.g., arterial line) should be dictated by comorbid conditions or if hemodynamic instability is expected for the procedure. When the degree of HPA axis is unknown and an expectant management is chosen regarding intraoperative supplemental steroid administration, vigilance is necessary for early detection of an adrenal crisis (distributive shock, hyperkalemia, hypoglycemia). If an intraoperative adrenal crisis occurs, hydrocortisone 100 mg intravenously is the drug of choice because it has mineralocorticoid activity; in addition, aggressively repleting the intravascular volume and using vasopressors as necessary. Dexamethasone has no mineralocorticoid activity and therefore should not be relied upon to prevent or treat an adrenal crisis. Etomidate should be avoided, but otherwise any anesthetic technique or agent is acceptable.

### Postoperative

Postoperatively, if perioperative supplemental steroids have been administered, doses should be tapered over the next few days as the stress of surgery subsides and the requirements diminish.

### CUSHING SYNDROME (GLUCOCORTICOID EXCESS)

Cushing syndrome is caused by ACTH-dependent or ACTHindependent processes. The most common cause of ACTHindependent glucocorticoid excess results from long- term treatment with glucocorticoids for conditions such as arthritis, asthma, and allergies. Adrenal tumors (adenoma or carcinoma) cause 10%–20% of spontaneous cases of ACTH- independent Cushing syndrome. ACTH- dependent causes include Cushing disease due to an ACTH-secreting pituitary microadenoma (65%–75% of spontaneous cases) or ectopic ACTH production (principally by tumors of the lung, pancreas, or thymus).<sup>99</sup>

Manifestations include a moon facies, dorsocervical fat pad (i.e. buffalo hump), plethoric build with a central distribution of fat (truncal obesity and skinny extremities), thin skin, easy bruising, and striae. Skeletal muscle wasting and muscle weakness are common, but the heart and diaphragm are usually spared. Patients may have difficulty



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 24 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 24 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0024.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 25 -->

climbing stairs. Patients may have hyperglycemia, and even diabetes mellitus from inhibition of peripheral use of glucose, anti- insulin action, and concomitant stimulation of gluconeogenesis. Hypertension and fluid retention are common, and osteopenia is a result of decreased formation of bone matrix and impaired absorption of calcium.<sup>100</sup>

The diagnosis is made by the clinical picture and confirmed by one of three tests: late night salivary cortisol, 24- hour free urinary cortisol, or an overnight 1 mg dexamethasone suppression test.

### Preoperative

Preoperative preparation of patients with Cushing syndrome focuses on optimizing comorbid conditions. Patients with Cushing syndrome are at risk for cardiovascular disease and stroke.<sup>101</sup> In addition, blood pressure and glucose control (or diabetes) should be optimized while ensuring intravascular volume and electrolyte values are normal.<sup>102,103</sup> Ectopic ACTH production may cause marked hypokalemic alkalosis and treatment with the aldosterone antagonist spironolactone stops the potassium loss and helps mobilize excess fluid.

### Intraoperative

In severe cases of Cushing syndrome, the moon facies and the dorsocervical fat pad portend difficulty with airway management and many patients have obstructive sleep apnea. Muscle relaxants may be used judiciously, but their action can be potentiated by excess steroids. Because of severe osteopenia and the risk of fractures, meticulous attention must be paid to positioning. Glucocorticoids are lympholytic and immunosuppressive, increasing the patient’s susceptibility to perioperative infection.<sup>104,105</sup> If adrenal resection is planned, supplemental glucocorticoids are administered at the start of tumor resection (100 mg of hydrocortisone every 24 hours).

### Postoperative

The tensile strength of healing wounds decreases in the presence of glucocorticoids, an effect that is at least partially reversed by the topical administration of vitamin A. Steroids are tapered over 3 to 6 days until a maintenance dose is reached. For patients undergoing bilateral resection, the patient should receive a mineralocorticoid, 9αfluorocortisol (0.05–0.1 mg/day), beginning on day 3. In certain patients, both steroids may require several adjustments. For a patient who has undergone unilateral adrenal resection, therapy is individualized according to the status of the remaining adrenal gland, and not all patients require mineralocorticoid replacement. Cushing syndrome patients are at risk for thromboembolic events and therefore appropriate perioperative prophylaxis should be instituted.<sup>106–108</sup>

### PHEOCHROMOCYTOMA

Pheochromocytomas and paragangliomas are catecholamine- producing tumors derived from chromaffin tissue of the adrenal and sympathetic chains, respectively. Historically, these tumors are associated with a high risk of perioperative mortality and morbidity ranging from 25% to 50%. Advancements in medical treatment and minimally invasive surgical techniques have led to a marked decrease

in perioperative mortality. Pheochromocytoma is known as the 10% disease as ∼10% are malignant (metastatic), 10% bilateral, and 10% extra- adrenal (paraganglioma). More recently it is recognized that all pheochromocytomas have malignant potential.<sup>109</sup> Pheochromocytomas occur as solitary tumors, or associated with diseases such as von Hippel–Lindau, neurofibromatosis type I, or multiple endocrine neoplasia type IIa or type IIb syndromes.

Signs and symptoms suggestive of pheochromocytoma such as sweating, palpitations, headaches, chest pain, tremors, weight loss, nausea, vomiting, and panic attacks result from catecholamine excess secretion. Spells consisting of a triad of episodic, paroxysmal headache, sweating, and tachycardia can be triggered by changes in position, anxiety, or medications. The diagnosis should be suspected in any young (especially less than 20 years of age) hypertensive patient, malignant or treatment- resistant hypertension, presence of classic “spells” even in the absence of hypertension, or in patients with familial syndromes associated with pheochromocytomas.

In patients with a low index of suspicion for a pheochromocytoma (such as those with uncontrolled hypertension), a 24- hour urinary fractionated metanephrine or catecholamine concentration has a specificity and sensitivity greater than 98%. In patients with a high index of suspicion (such as those with an adrenal mass, or hypertensive with one of the familial syndromes associated with a pheochromocytoma), plasma fractionated metanephrines or catecholamines are the tests of choice with a sensitivity of 96%–100%, and a specificity of 85%–89%. Plasma and 24hour free metanephrine concentrations above two times the upper limit of normal are diagnostic for pheochromocytoma.<sup>110</sup> A clonidine suppression test may correctly tease out the false-positive borderline elevated plasma metanephrine concentrations.<sup>111</sup> A positive screening test is followed by localization of the tumor with MRI, CT, or metaiodobenzylguanidine nuclear scanning.

### Preoperative

The focus is on treating hypertension and restoring intravascular volume. Optimizing end-organ sequelae such as catecholamine- induced cardiomyopathy, tachyarrhythmias, and acute coronary syndrome is important.<sup>112</sup> Traditionally, hypertensive treatment relied on the Roizen criteria described in the 1980s: (1) achieving a preoperative blood pressure less than 165/90 mm Hg; (2) presence of orthostatic hypotension as long as the blood pressure is greater than 80/45 mm Hg; (3) no ST- T segment changes on the ECG; and (4) fewer than 1 premature ventricular contraction in 5 minutes. Based on retrospective data, the 2014 Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline challenged the Roizen criteria by recommending tighter blood pressure control of less than 130/80 mm Hg while sitting (systolic blood pressure greater than 90 mm Hg when standing), aiming for a heart rate 60–70 beats/min while sitting (70–80 beats/min when standing).<sup>113</sup>

Blood pressure control is primarily with alpha (α) blockers. The nonselective alpha blocker phenoxybenzamine is most commonly used with doses starting at 10 mg twice daily and slowly titrated to achieve the desired blood pressure control. Selective α1- selective blockers such as prazosin (2–5 mg, 2–3 times daily), doxazosin (2–8 mg daily), and

<!-- PDF page 26 -->

terazosin (2–5 mg daily) have gained popularity recently given better safety profiles and lower costs.<sup>114</sup> The optimal strategy of using alpha blocking is unclear. However, patients treated preoperatively with nonselective alpha blockers may have less intraoperative hypertension compared to those treated with selective alpha blockade.<sup>115,116</sup> Another small study detected more postoperative hypotension requiring vasopressor support in patients with selective alpha blockade compared with nonselective agents.<sup>117</sup> A recent meta-analysis showed that nonselective alpha blockade was more effective in preventing intraoperative blood pressure fluctuations while maintaining comparable risks of both intra- , and postoperative hypotension and overall morbidity. Similar results were confirmed in a randomized prospective trial comparing phenoxybenzamine with doxazosin.<sup>118,119</sup> Current guidelines endorse an optimal duration of alpha blockade between 7 and 14 days preoperatively. Preoperative duration of alpha blockade for more than 30 days is associated with intraoperative bradycardia and postoperative hypotension requiring vasopressor support.<sup>120</sup> In spite of guideline endorsement of alpha blockade, recent evidence challenges its value and utility.<sup>121</sup> A metaanalysis of 603 patients showed that mortality, cardiovascular complications, mean maximal intraoperative systolic and diastolic blood pressures, and mean maximal intraoperative heart rates did not differ between patients with or without alpha blockade.<sup>122</sup> Another recent small study showed that hemodynamic instability remained significant despite optimal alpha and beta blockade.<sup>123</sup> Moreover, in an international multicenter study of 1860 patients, mortality was not improved by preoperative alpha blockade and complications were more frequent (5% vs. 0.9%) than in patients without alpha blockade.<sup>124</sup>

If persistent tachycardia is present or the target heart rate is not achieved, beta blockers are typically added to alpha blockade. Beta blockers are started only after adequate alpha blockade to prevent worsening hypertension due to the blockade of vasodilatory peripheral β- adrenergic receptors and unopposed alpha stimulation.<sup>113,114</sup> Calcium channel blockers are indicated for patients not tolerating alpha blockade because of profound orthostasis or those needing additional blood pressure control. Nicardipine, amlodipine, and nifedipine are the most frequently used. Amlodipine can be an efficacious alternative to alpha blockade to prevent hemodynamic instability during surgical resection.<sup>125,126</sup> The Endocrine Society Task Force guidelines further recommend a high sodium diet (3–5 g/day) and fluid intake to reverse the catecholamine- induced volume contraction during alpha blockade.<sup>113</sup> Preoperative intravenous volume repletion does not optimize hemodynamics or improve outcomes.<sup>127</sup> Metyrosine (a tyrosine hydroxylase inhibitor) decreases the circulating catecholamine concentrations by 50% to 80%.<sup>114</sup> It can be added for symptomatic control in patients with high catecholamine concentrations or when blood pressure control is not achieved with the approach described above. In general, due to profound sedation, it is reserved for symptomatic control in patients with metastatic pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma.

### Intraoperative

Most pheochromocytomas are resected using minimally invasive techniques such as laparoscopic assisted or

retroperitoneoscopic approaches. However, large tumors may necessitate an open procedure. Preinduction anxiolysis with benzodiazepines or dexmedetomidine is beneficial in reducing catecholamine surges. In addition to adequate intravenous access, an arterial line is paramount in all pheochromocytoma resection cases. The arterial line is generally placed preinduction to facilitate immediate blood pressure control. Central venous access is considered when patients are expected to need postoperative vasoactive infusions but should not be routinely placed. Additional monitoring with transesophageal echocardiography or pulmonary artery catheters may be useful in patients with ventricular dysfunction. A slow, controlled induction and intubation only after achieving an adequate anesthetic depth minimizes hemodynamic instability. All anesthetic techniques are acceptable as long as hemodynamic control is achieved. Commonly used induction agents such as propofol or etomidate are safe, but ketamine should be avoided due to sympathetic effects. Inhalational anesthetics (with the exception of desflurane which can cause transient tachycardia), neuromuscular blocking agents (with the exception of pancuronium), and most opioids have been successfully used during pheochromocytoma resection. Epidural anesthesia in addition to general anesthesia may be beneficial for postoperative pain control, but it may not attenuate intraoperative hypertensive episodes and can lead to postoperative hypotension.<sup>128,129</sup>

Anesthesia management focuses on hemodynamic control and good communication among members of the perioperative team. Catecholamine crises are expected before tumor removal (vein ligation) and can be precipitated by induction of anesthesia, medications (e.g., metoclopramide), or surgical manipulation. Moreover, hemodynamic instability is more common with large tumor sizes, elevated urinary epinephrine or norepinephrine concentrations, low body mass index, and coronary artery disease.<sup>130,131</sup>

During the pre- vein ligation phase, short- acting vasoactive drugs are used to control and prevent hypertensive episodes. Clevidipine, nitroglycerin, nitroprusside, and nicardipine are preferred. Esmolol can be administered for heart rate control. Phentolamine, the typical intravenous alpha blocker, has a prolonged onset and duration of action and is rarely used in clinical practice. Intravascular volume loading is necessary to minimize post- vein ligation hypotension. Hypertensive episodes are common during induction, intubation, and positioning, and are due to catecholamine release from nerve endings. In comparison, surgical manipulation with compression of the tumor is accompanied by further intravascular discharge of catecholamines and an exaggerated hypertensive response. In addition to the traditional vasoactive substances, magnesium, a direct vasodilator, is commonly used during pheochromocytoma resection to provide hemodynamic stability by stabilizing membranes and decreasing the release of catecholamine directly from the tumor.<sup>132–134</sup> It is usually administered in a 2–4 g loading dose before induction of anesthesia followed by a 1–2 g/h infusion during resection.

Post- vein ligation, normalization of blood pressure is expected if intravascular volume is normal, and all vasodilators can be discontinued. However, some patients may become hypotensive and occasionally require catecholamine infusions. Phenylephrine is the initial drug of

<!-- PDF page 27 -->

choice to treat hypotension due to its ease of use, although some patients may require norepinephrine or epinephrine infusions. Generally, the choice of vasopressor is based on the type of catecholamine predominantly secreted by the tumor (e.g., norepinephrine for a patient with elevated preoperative normetanephrines). For patients refractory to alpha agonists, vasopressin and methylene blue can be effective.<sup>135</sup>

Hyperglycemia can occur because excess catecholamines increase insulin resistance, compromise insulin secretion, and decrease glucose uptake even in nondiabetic patients.<sup>136</sup> Intraoperative hyperglycemia correction is done judiciously, as postoperative normalization of glucose and even hypoglycemia are expected and can be aggravated by residual insulin.

### Postoperative

Patients are generally monitored in an intensive care setting. However, a select group of patients having minimally invasive resection who are hemodynamically stable can be discharged to the regular ward.<sup>137</sup> Postoperative considerations include hemodynamic monitoring (as some patients may require pressors) and close glucose monitoring. After pheochromocytoma resection, there is a rebound hyperinsulinemia with hypoglycemia especially in patients with pheochromocytomas excreting epinephrine or after prolonged resections.<sup>138</sup> Glucose concentrations should be monitored every 1–2 hours for the first 6–24 hours. Approximately 50% of patients remain hypertensive for 1 to 3 days and initially have markedly increased but declining plasma catecholamine concentrations – after which all but 25% will become normotensive.

### CONN SYNDROME (MINERALOCORTICOID EXCESS)

Primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn syndrome) most often results from a unilateral adenoma, although 25% to 40% of patients have bilateral adrenal hyperplasia. Excess mineralocorticoid activity leads to sodium retention, potassium depletion, muscle weakness, hypertension, tetany, polyuria with inability to concentrate urine, and hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis.<sup>139</sup> Primary hyperaldosteronism is suspected in patients with hypertension and hypokalemia, treatment resistance (more than 3 medications), adrenal incidentalomas, or severe or early onset hypertension (younger than 30 years old). The diagnosis is confirmed by measuring the plasma aldosterone concentration (greater than 10 ng/dL) as well as plasma renin activity (less than 1 ng/ml/h).<sup>140</sup>

### Preoperative

Preparation relies on treatment of hypertension and restoring the intravascular fluid volume and electrolyte concentrations (correcting hypokalemia with supplemental potassium). In addition, there is evidence of increased rates of metabolic syndrome and glucose intolerance in these patients and this should be determined preoperatively.<sup>141</sup> Renal function and electrolyte abnormalities are corrected by administering the aldosterone antagonist spironolactone. The effects of spironolactone are slow in onset and increase for 1 to 2 weeks. Frequently, a period of at least 24 hours is required to restore potassium equilibrium as

the deficit can be up to 400 mEq. However, normal serum potassium concentration does not necessarily imply correction of a total- body deficit of potassium. In addition, patients with Conn syndrome have a high incidence of ischemic heart disease and preoperative evaluation should be based on current guidelines.<sup>142</sup>

### Intraoperative

Monitoring is tailored to the individual patient but invasive monitoring (e.g., arterial line) is not usually necessary when the preoperative preparation is adequate. Perioperative glucocorticoid supplementation is not indicated in patients with Conn syndrome undergoing adrenalectomy.

### HYPERTHYROIDISM

Hyperthyroidism is usually due to Graves disease, toxic multinodular goiter, or a thyroid adenoma. It can also occur with pregnancy, thyroiditis, choriocarcinoma, or due to a thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)- secreting pituitary adenoma. The diagnosis of hyperthyroidism in the setting of Graves disease or multinodular goiter relies on a low TSH with elevated concentrations of triiodothyronine (T3) (the active hormone) and thyroxine (T4). Common manifestations of hyperthyroidism include anxiety, weight loss, diarrhea, warm and moist skin, weakness of large muscle groups, osteopenia, intolerance to heat, palpitations, tachycardia, cardiac arrhythmias, and high-output heart failure.<sup>143a</sup> In addition, patients with Graves disease (autoimmune disease due to TSH-binding antibodies leading to enlargement of the thyroid gland and hypervascularity) present with lid retraction, wide stare, and exophthalmos as well as dermatologic complications. The goiter is diffusely enlarged and compressive symptoms (stridor, dyspnea, dysphagia) are possible. Multinodular goiters typically present as slow- growing asymmetric growth nodules that can become very large. Patients usually present with symptoms consistent with compression of the trachea, esophagus, or even superior vena cava through extension into the mediastinum, as well as moderate hyperthyroidism features. Subclinical hyperthyroidism (normal serum T3 and T4 with low TSH [&lt;0.5. mU/L]) may be due to exogenous or endogenous causes. Most patients have only mild symptoms (if any) but have increased risk of atrial fibrillation, heart failure, coronary artery disease, bone loss, fractures, dementia, and progression to overt hyperthyroidism.<sup>143b</sup>

### Preoperative

Preparation of patients with hyperthyroidism relies on controlling the symptoms as well as evaluating end-organ effects (e.g., presence of arrhythmias or heart failure) and the degree of airway compression for patients with goiters. Antithyroid medications are the mainstay of therapy for patients with hyperthyroidism and include propylthiouracil and methimazole, both of which decrease the synthesis of T4 and may enhance remission by reducing TSH receptor antibody concentrations (the primary pathologic mechanism in Graves disease). Propylthiouracil also decreases the conversion of T4 to the more potent T3. Generally, antithyroid medications control the disease in 6–8 weeks. Beta blockers do not affect thyroid function but help control some of the hyperadrenergic symptoms associated with the

<!-- PDF page 28 -->

disease (e.g., arrhythmias, tremors). Propranolol is the beta blocker of choice given that, it further prevents the conversion of T4 to T3 (active hormone). Patients who are refractory to medical therapy and need rapid control of symptoms in preparation for thyroidectomy can benefit from iodide administration. Generally, a saturated solution of potassium iodide (SSKI) blocks the synthesis and discharge of thyroid hormones from the gland and quickly controls the symptoms (7–10 days) as compared to 2–6 weeks with a traditional antithyroid medication–only approach. Iodidebased contrast agent such as iopanoic acid may have similar effects. Regardless of the approach, antithyroid drugs should be administered on a long- term basis and on the morning of the surgical procedure.

Patients with subclinical and well-controlled hyperthyroidism have increased risks of arrhythmias, especially atrial fibrillation, and MACE. Patients who are overtly hyperthyroid (symptomatic) should have their disease optimized with antithyroid medications as well as iodide if preoperative preparation time is limited. A good indication of control before surgery relies on normalization of free T4 concentrations and lack of overt hyperthyroid symptoms, as TSH may lag 4–6 weeks behind and may still be suppressed. Only emergency operations or thyroidectomy planned to control the disease that does not respond to maximal medical treatment should proceed in patients with uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, due to a high risk of developing intraoperative thyroid storm. Although the risk of thyroid storm is difficult to quantify, new evidence suggests that even when patients do not tolerate antithyroid medications, thyroidectomy can be performed without increased risk of thyroid storm and complications.<sup>144,145</sup> For patients with large goiters, a thorough evaluation of the airway as well as a review of the neck CT scan should inform decisions on day of surgery airway management.<sup>146</sup>

### Intraoperative

Thyroid function does not affect the minimal alveolar concentration of inhalational anesthetics and all are acceptable. Sympathetic stimulants (such as ketamine, pancuronium, anticholinergic drugs) may be avoided as they can aggravate tachycardia. Propofol and inhalational anesthetics are safe. Clearance and distribution volume of propofol are increased in patients with hyperthyroidism. When total intravenous anesthesia is used, propofol infusion rates should be increased to reach anesthetic blood concentrations. Induction of anesthesia may be slower than in euthyroid patients and may require increased concentrations of volatile anesthetics because of elevated cardiac output in patients with hyperthyroidism. The anesthetic depth should be maintained to minimize sympathetic reactions. Invasive monitoring should be individualized based on hemodynamic preoperative stability as well as the proposed procedure. The cardiovascular system is most at risk in patients with uncontrolled hyperthyroidism. Patients with hyperthyroidism have systolic hypertension, with a widened pulse pressure and a decrease in systemic vascular resistance.<sup>147,148</sup> Cardiac manifestations are atrial fibrillation, sinus tachycardia, or ultimately, high-output heart failure.<sup>149</sup> Patients with hyperthyroidism may have increased sensitivity to catecholamines. The traditional recommendation is to use direct-acting vasoconstrictors

(e.g., phenylephrine) rather than indirect acting medications (e.g., ephedrine). However, this is controversial. Some _in vitro_ human studies have reported an increase in cardiac β- adrenergic receptor density in patients with hyperthyroidism. However, there is evidence that the increased receptor density may be balanced by reduced adrenergic receptor responsiveness to catecholamines.

When diarrhea is severe, the associated dehydration and electrolyte abnormalities can lead to hypovolemia and should ideally be corrected perioperatively. Beta blockers are the mainstay of therapy for patients manifesting symptoms of hyperthyroidism intraoperatively. While beta blockers offer only symptomatic treatment, they do not prevent a thyroid storm.<sup>150</sup> Although β- adrenergic receptor blockade can control the heart rate, its use is challenging in the setting of heart failure. However, a controlled heart rate may improve cardiac filling with diastolic dysfunction. Patients with hyperthyroidism with rapid ventricular rates, and heart failure requiring emergency surgery can be safely given shortacting beta blockers guided by clinical response. If slowing the heart rate with a small dose of esmolol (0.1–0.2 mg/kg) does not aggravate the heart failure, the infusion should be titrated to the desired heart rate using doses of 50–350 mcg/kg/h.

Patients with large goiters and an obstructed airway can be managed in the same way as any other patient with a problematic airway. Compression from thyroid nodules generally leads to lateral compression of the trachea in contrast to other anterior mediastinal masses. As such, an awake fiberoptic approach to securing the airway as well as avoidance of muscle relaxation due to fear of airway collapse is rarely needed unless other signs of difficult laryngoscopy or ventilation are present. In patients with long- standing goiters, extubation should be performed under optimal circumstances for reintubation in the event that tracheomalacia is present.

### Postoperative

Complications include nerve injury, bleeding, metabolic abnormalities, thyroid storm, bilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve trauma, and hypocalcemic tetany. Bilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury (secondary to surgical trauma or edema) causes stridor and laryngeal obstruction as a result of unopposed adduction of the vocal cords and closure of the glottic aperture. Immediate endotracheal intubation is required, usually followed by tracheostomy. Unilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury often goes unnoticed because of compensatory over- adduction of the uninvolved cord. Unilateral nerve injury is characterized by hoarseness, whereas aphonia characterizes bilateral nerve injury. Selective injury to the adductor fibers of both recurrent laryngeal nerves leaves the abductor muscles relatively unopposed, and pulmonary aspiration is a risk. Selective injury to the abductor muscles leaves the adductor muscles relatively unopposed, and airway obstruction can occur.

The intimate involvement of the parathyroid gland with the thyroid gland can result in inadvertent hypocalcemia during surgery for thyroid disease. Complications related to hypocalcemia are discussed in other sections of this chapter. Postoperative hematoma can cause immediate compromise of the airway. Opening the surgical incision in the postoperative anesthesia care unit is considered, rather than waiting to return to the operating room, as a delay may cause acute airway compression.<sup>151</sup>

<!-- PDF page 29 -->

### THYROID STORM

Thyroid storm is a life- threatening complication in a patient whose hyperthyroidism has been severely exacerbated by illness, trauma, or surgery. Thyroid storm is characterized by hyperthermia, tachycardia, and striking alterations in consciousness. Its clinical appearance is similar to malignant hyperthermia, pheochromocytoma, and neuroleptic malignant syndrome, further complicating the differential.

### Preoperative

No laboratory tests are diagnostic of thyroid storm. Therapy relies on blocking the synthesis of thyroid hormones by administering antithyroid drugs and preventing the release of preformed hormone with iodine. Beta blockers (esmolol infusion) are the mainstay of therapy for controlling tachycardia along with supportive measures (cooling blankets and ice packs to control pyrexia or fluids to replete the intravascular volume). Hydrocortisone administration treats concurrent adrenal insufficiency and blocks the conversion of T4 to T3. Concomitant atrial fibrillation should be treated, but amiodarone is avoided as it can cause thyroid dysfunction (hypo- or hyperthyroidism). When thyroid storm occurs outside the operating room (e.g., intensive care unit), additional therapies may be available such as bile acid sequestrants, lithium, or even plasmapheresis.<sup>152–154</sup> Despite maximal medical therapy, mortality from thyroid storm remains high (10%).<sup>155</sup> The risk of precipitating thyroid storm in patients treated preoperatively with antithyroid medications is very low.

### Intraoperative

Only emergency surgeries should be undertaken in patients with or at high risk of thyroid storm. During anesthesia, thyroid storm is difficult to differentiate from malignant hyperthermia. Beta blockers (esmolol infusion), active cooling, correction of metabolic derangements, acetaminophen, and hydrocortisone are used to control tachycardia, hyperthermia, and other manifestations, including adrenal insufficiency. Further treatment includes antithyroid medications (methimazole or propylthiouracil), administered under the direction of an endocrinologist. Invasive monitoring and preparation to manage heart failure and arrhythmias are necessary. Drugs that activate the sympathetic nervous system or that have indirect mechanisms, such as ephedrine, are used judiciously or avoided. The temperature should be monitored for early signs of thyroid storm. The same principles that apply to the anesthetic management of patients with hyperthyroidism apply to these patients.

### Postoperative

Thyroid storm has been reported during surgery and in the first 18 hours after thyroid and non- thyroid surgery in hyperthyroid patients. Monitoring for signs of thyroid storm, resuming medications, and treating pain are important for these patients.

### HYPOTHYROIDISM

Hypothyroidism is a common disease characterized by a decrease in secretion of thyroid hormones from the thyroid gland despite elevated concentrations of TSH. The most common causes of primary hypothyroidism are resulting from

radioactive iodine treatment for Graves disease and Hashimoto thyroiditis (an autoimmune disease mediated by autoantibodies to TSH receptors in the thyroid gland). A common laboratory feature is characterized by low concentrations of free T3 and T4 with concomitant increased concentrations of TSH. Secondary hypothyroidism is due to decreased concentrations of thyroid releasing hormone (TRH) from the pituitary and low concentrations of TSH, T3, and T4. Euthyroid sick syndrome is a difficult entity to tease out, characterized by hypothyroidism features in patients with critical illness. As compared with patients with clinical hypothyroidism, patients with euthyroid sick syndrome have low concentrations of T3 and T4 and near-normal concentrations of TSH. No treatment is necessary and it resolves upon amelioration of the underlying triggering disease.

Symptoms and signs of hypothyroidism include fatigue, slowing of reflexes, constipation, and dry thickened skin. Generally, the symptoms are insidious and nonspecific in patients with mild hypothyroidism. Patients with more severe forms present with a large tongue, hoarse voice, and cardiac and respiratory symptoms (Table 29.11). Myxedema coma is a rare complication of severe, untreated hypothyroidism usually precipitated by trauma, infection, myocardial infarction, surgery, or other critical illnesses.<sup>150</sup> Patients present with altered mentation (although usually not coma) and possibly seizures (due to concurrent hyponatremia), hypothermia, bradycardia, hypotension, hypoventilation, hyponatremia, and hypoglycemia. Myxedema coma has a high mortality rate (30%–50%).

### Preoperative

Preparation of the hypothyroid patient consists of restoring thyroid function. Ideally patients should have normal thyroid function before elective surgery. However, those with mild hypothyroidism may have low risk of complications. Patients with severe hypothyroidism need to have their disease controlled due to risks associated with cardiac and respiratory decompensation.<sup>143a,156</sup>

**Table 29.11** Manifestations of Hypothyroidism by Organ System

|**Organ**|**Symptoms**|
|---|---|
|General|Apathy, fatigue, weight gain|
|Skin|Sweating, discoloration, brittle hair, nonpitting<br>edema, vitiligo|
|Eyes|Periorbital edema|
|Cardiac|Cardiomyopathy, pericardial effusion,<br>hypertension, hypercholesterolemia|
|Respiratory|Hypoventilation<br>Reduced response to hypoxia and hypercarbia|
|Gastrointestinal|Constipation, decreased taste sensation<br>Ileus<br>Gastric atrophy<br>Non- alcoholic hepatic steatosis<br>Celiac disease|
|Hematologic|Anemia, bleeding due to acquired von Willebrand<br>syndrome|
|Neurologic|Cognitive dysfunction, carpal tunnel syndrome,<br>peripheral neuropathy, myopathy|



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 29 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 29 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0029.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 30 -->

Thyroid replacement hormones should be continued preoperatively, including on the day of surgery. For patients requiring rapid symptom control, intravenous administration of T4 thyroid hormone is preferred at doses of 50–100 mcg/ day following an initial loading dose of 200–500 mcg or faster control can be achieved using liothyronine (T3). These doses need to be reduced and carefully titrated in patients with ischemic heart disease because rapid replacement of thyroid hormones at usual doses may exacerbate ischemia. Patients with myxedema coma should be treated with a combination of intravenous T4 and T3 for rapid restoration of thyroid hormone concentrations, hydrocortisone for possible concurrent adrenal insufficiency, volume restoration, and treatment of the triggering pathology.

### Intraoperative

Anesthesia management has few implications in patients with mild to moderate hypothyroidism. For patients with severe disease or patients with myxedema undergoing emergency procedures, airway management can be problematic as patients can present with enlarged tongues, clinical features of obstructive sleep apnea, or vocal cord edema. Severely hypothyroid patients can be at increased risk of aspiration. Therefore, a rapid-sequence induction may be indicated. Clinicians must be vigilant for cardiac alterations posed by severe hypothyroidism such as reduced cardiac output, arrhythmias (bradycardia, slow rate atrial fibrillation, prolonged QTc leading to ventricular arrhythmias) and decreased intravascular volume with narrow pulse pressures. These can be exacerbated by the cardiodepressant and vasodilatory effects of inhalational and intravenous anesthetic agents, leading to hypotension. In patients with severe hypothyroidism, intravascular volume should be maintained with attention to cardiac decompensation. Patients may have ventilatory muscle weakness and a reduced response to hypoxia and hypercarbia. There may be sensitive to anesthetic agents that exacerbate these effects (such as narcotics and sedatives). Patients can have delayed emergence and prolonged postoperative sedation. Long- acting sedating agents should be dosed judiciously. Coagulopathy due to an acquired von Willebrand syndrome can lead to increased bleeding, and electrolyte abnormalities (hyponatremia, hypoglycemia) should be corrected.<sup>157</sup>

### Postoperative

Obstructive sleep apnea is more common in patients with hypothyroidism. Clinicians should be aware of possible prolonged effects of sedating medications used intraoperatively.

### HYPERPARATHYROIDISM AND HYPERCALCEMIA

Primary hyperparathyroidism usually results from enlargement of a single gland, commonly an adenoma, and more rarely from multiglandular disease. Parathyroid hormone (PTH) controls calcium concentrations, and in hyperparathyroidism, hypercalcemia almost always develops. Many of the prominent symptoms of hyperparathyroidism are a result of the hypercalcemia. Regardless of the cause, hypercalcemia can produce any number of symptoms, the most prominent of which involve the renal, skeletal, neuromuscular, and

gastrointestinal (GI) systems (Table 29.12). The diagnosis of hyperparathyroidism is confirmed by an elevated or normal concentration of PTH in a hypercalcemic patient.

Hypercalcemia may also occur as a consequence of secondary hyperparathyroidism in patients who have chronic renal disease. When phosphate excretion decreases as a result of decreased nephron mass, serum calcium concentrations fall because of deposition of calcium and phosphate in bone. Secretion of PTH subsequently increases, and this causes the fraction of phosphate excreted by each nephron to increase. Eventually, the chronic intermittent hypocalcemia of chronic renal failure leads to chronically high concentrations of serum PTH and hyperplasia of the parathyroid glands – one of the entities termed secondary hyperparathyroidism.<sup>158</sup> The normal total serum calcium concentration is 8.6 to 10.4 mg/dL, a value that depends on the albumin concentration, with a decline of 0.8 mg/ dL for each 1 g/dL drop in albumin. Binding of calcium to albumin depends on pH: binding decreases with acidic pH and increases with alkaline pH. Serum calcium, not ionized calcium, decreases with reductions in albumin concentrations. Although ionized calcium is the clinically significant fraction, the cost and technical difficulties of stabilizing the electrodes used for measurement have limited the available assays. Nevertheless, PTH and vitamin D3 keep calcium concentrations stable within 0.1 mg/dL in any individual.

### Preoperative

Preparation of the patient with hyperparathyroidism relies on controlling the underlying disease (such as in malignancyassociated hypercalcemia, parathyroidectomy for primary hyperparathyroidism) and, if not possible, decreasing calcium concentrations preoperatively. Generally, patients with mild to moderate hypercalcemia who have normal renal and cardiovascular function require no special preoperative preparation. An ECG is done to look for prolonged

**Table 29.12** Symptoms of Hypercalcemia (Hyperparathyroidism)

|**Organ**|**Symptoms**|
|---|---|
|Renal|Nephrolithiasis (60%–70%)<br>Renal tubular acidosis<br>Polyuria, polydipsia|
|Musculoskeletal|Osteitis fibrosa cystica<br>Diffuse osteopenia/osteoporosis<br>Bone pain<br>Muscle weakness/atrophy|
|Neurologic/psychiatric|Depression<br>Psychomotor retardation<br>Memory impairment<br>Lethargy<br>Confusion<br>Seizures|
|Gastrointestinal|Anorexia, vomiting, and constipation<br>Peptic ulcer disease (increased gastrin)<br>Pancreatitis|
|Cardiovascular|Calcifications in the myocardium<br>Hypertension<br>ECG changes – prolonged PR, shortened<br>QT intervals|


_ECG_ , electrocardiogram.



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 30 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 30 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0030.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 31 -->

PR or shortened QT intervals. Because severe hypercalcemia can result in substantial hypovolemia, normal intravascular volume and electrolyte status should be evaluated and then restored before anesthesia and surgery. Therapy for both malignant and nonmalignant causes of severe hypercalcemia include aggressive volume repletion, with the addition of diuretics only if volume overload develops. Intravenous fluid infusion rates of 250 to 500 mL/h preoperatively are commonly used to maintain urine output greater than 200 mL/h. Careful monitoring is needed to avoid administering an excessive volume of intravenous fluids, as many patients may have compromised cardiac function. The addition of furosemide may be needed to promote hypercalciuria.

In emergency situations, vigorous expansion of intravascular volume usually reduces serum calcium to a safe concentration (less than 14 mg/dL). Phosphate should be given to correct hypophosphatemia because hypophosphatemia will decrease calcium uptake into bone, increase calcium excretion, and stimulate breakdown of bone. Hydration, accompanied by electrolyte, especially phosphate repletion, suffices in the management of most hypercalcemic patients. Other measures useful in patients with malignant hypercalcemia are meant to decrease reabsorption of bone and include the bisphosphonates pamidronate sodium (90 mg intravenously) and zoledronate (4 mg intravenously). Additionally, calcitonin lowers serum calcium concentrations through direct inhibition of bone resorption. It can decrease serum calcium concentrations within minutes after intravenous administration. Side effects include urticaria and nausea. It is so rapid acting that it can be used to reduce calcium concentrations while waiting for hydration and a bisphosphonate to take effect. Dialysis should be reserved for patients with renal failure. Serious cardiac, renal, or CNS abnormalities may develop with chronic hypercalcemia and need to be optimized preoperatively.

### Intraoperative

Moderately elevated calcium concentrations do not pose problems. Some patients with skeletal effects of chronic hypercalcemia need careful positioning to prevent pathologic fractures. High calcium concentrations can antagonize the effect of neuromuscular blocking agents (NMBAs), thus careful monitoring of neuromuscular function is required intraoperatively. The ECG is monitored for prolonged PR or shortened QT intervals. Usually, the recurrent laryngeal nerve is monitored by the surgical team during neck dissection, so NMBAs are avoided.

### Postoperative

There are few concerns related to calcium concentrations postoperatively. After parathyroidectomy, assessment of vocal cord function is advocated. Vocal cord function is easily assessed by instructing the patient to say “e.”

### HYPOPARATHYROIDISM AND HYPOCALCEMIA

Hypoparathyroidism (usually due to inadvertent removal of parathyroid glands during thyroidectomy) and pseudohypoparathyroidism (the kidneys do not respond to the action of intact PTH) are uncommon causes of hypocalcemia. Mostly, hypocalcemia is due to hypoalbuminemia or as a result of other metabolic abnormalities (hypomagnesemia,

hypovitaminosis D, hungry bone syndrome after correction of hyperparathyroidism, anticonvulsant therapy, citrate infusion, or chronic renal disease). True hypocalcemia (i.e., when the free calcium concentration is low) results in depressed myocardial contractility. Myocardial contractility varies directly with concentrations of blood ionized calcium. The clinical signs of hypocalcemia include clumsiness, convulsions, laryngeal stridor, depression, muscle stiffness, paresthesias, parkinsonism, tetany, Chvostek sign, dry and scaly skin, brittle nails, coarse hair, low serum concentrations of calcium, prolonged QT intervals, soft tissue calcifications, and Trousseau sign.

Hypocalcemia delays ventricular repolarization, hence increasing the QTc interval. Heart failure may occur with hypocalcemia but is rare. Patients with hypocalcemia may have seizures, often indistinguishable from epilepsy in the absence of hypocalcemia. Patients may also have a type of seizure called cerebral tetany, which consists of generalized tetany followed by tonic spasms.

### Preoperative

The objective is to bring the symptoms under control before the surgical procedure and anesthesia. For patients with chronic hypoparathyroidism, the aim is to keep the serum calcium concentration in the lower half of the normal range by giving oral calcium and vitamin D. A preoperative ECG is useful for determining the QTc interval.

Patients with symptomatic hypocalcemia may be treated with intravenous calcium gluconate. Initially, 10 to 20 mL of 10% calcium gluconate may be given at a rate of 5 mL/ min, followed by a continuous infusion of 10% calcium gluconate in 500 mL of fluid over a period of 6 hours to keep serum calcium at adequate concentrations. For severe symptoms in emergency settings, 10 mL of 10% calcium chloride may be given over 10 minutes, followed by a 10% calcium gluconate infusion. Magnesium and phosphate concentrations may need to be corrected to normalize cardiovascular and nervous system functions. Heart failure in patients with coexisting heart disease improves when calcium and magnesium ion concentrations are restored to normal. Magnesium and phosphate concentrations should be corrected in a patient with impaired exercise tolerance or signs of cardiovascular dysfunction. Normalization can be achieved intravenously over a 15- minute period if necessary.

### Intraoperative

The same measures should be undertaken intraoperatively to maintain calcium concentrations close to normal, as sudden decreases in blood concentrations of ionized calcium (as with chelation therapy) can result in severe hypotension. Thus, alkalosis, whether respiratory (hyperventilation) or metabolic (bicarbonate administration) is avoided to decrease the risk of further decline in calcium concentrations. Similarly, citrate administered in large quantities during massive transfusion protocols can lower ionized calcium concentrations. Changes in the calcium concentration may impact NMBAs. Careful monitoring and titration of medications with a train- of- four monitor is necessary.

### Postoperative

Calcium concentrations should be monitored similar to the other phases of care.

<!-- PDF page 32 -->

### ACROMEGALY

Acromegaly is caused by an excess of growth hormone (GH), usually due to a pituitary adenoma (99% of time). The diagnosis is suspected when physical features consistent with the disease appear. It is confirmed by elevated serum insulin-like growth factor-1 (IGF-1) concentrations or by a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test followed by growth hormone measurement (a concentration greater than 1 ng/mL is diagnostic). Clinical features of acromegaly include headaches, visual field deficits, and cranial nerve palsies associated with the pituitary-secreting adenoma as well as possible signs related to other pituitary hormone deficiency (most commonly gonadotropins). These symptoms resolve with surgical or medical treatment in patients where surgical resection is not feasible. Other manifestations of GH excess manifest as cardiovascular disease, hypertension, diabetes, glucose intolerance, and obstructive sleep apnea, and may resolve with treatment. Overgrowth of tissues due to GH may persist despite treatment of acromegaly<sup>159,160</sup> (Table 29.13)

Treatment consists of transphenoidal resection of the pituitary adenoma, or if the pituitary tumor is not totally removed, patients are often offered external pituitary irradiation. In the case of suprasellar extension, conventional transfrontal hypophysectomy is often performed. Medical therapy with cabergoline (dopaminergic agonist), somatostatin analogue (octreotide), or pegvisomant (blocks GH action) can be used in patients with incomplete resections. There is some evidence that somatostatin receptor ligand medication taken preoperatively may result in a better postoperative outcome.

### Preoperative

Comorbidities (hypertension, cardiovascular disease, diabetes) need to be evaluated and optimized. A careful assessment of the airway is important. Lateral neck radiographs or CT scans of the neck and direct or indirect laryngoscopy can identify patients with subglottic stenosis or an enlarged tongue, mandible, epiglottis, or vocal cords.

### Intraoperative

Difficulty with securing the airway is common in a patient with acromegaly. Mask ventilation may be difficult due to overgrowth of the mandible, distorted facial anatomy, and pharyngeal soft tissues. Moreover, laryngoscopy can be

**Table 29.13** Manifestations of Acromegaly

|**Organ**|**Signs/Symptoms**|
|---|---|
|Cardiovascular|Hypertension<br>Cardiomegaly<br>Cardiomyopathy<br>Valvular disease|
|Skin/tissue overgrowth|Macroglossia<br>Deepening of voice<br>Macrognathia<br>Carpal tunnel syndrome<br>Obstructive sleep apnea<br>Hypertrophic arthropathies|
|Metabolic|Insulin resistance<br>Diabetes<br>Hyperphosphatemia|


impeded by an enlarged tongue and epiglottis and longer distance from the lips to the vocal cords. Endotracheal intubation can be problematic due to possible subglottic stenosis, requiring a smaller than normal endotracheal tube. If placement of an arterial line is necessary, a brachial, axillary, or femoral site may be preferable to a radial site, as there can be compromise in the collateral circulation of the wrist.<sup>104</sup>

### Postoperative

Care should be taken when extubating a patient who required an advanced technique for securing the airway.

### DIABETES INSIPIDUS (HYPERNATREMIA)

Antidiuretic hormone is produced in the supraoptic nuclei of the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior pituitary. Diabetes insipidus (DI) results either from destruction of the posterior pituitary or resection of the pituitary stalk during trauma or neurosurgical procedures (central DI) or from inability of the renal collecting tubules to respond to the action of antidiuretic hormone (nephrogenic DI). Regardless of the cause, the main features of DI are polydipsia (in an awake patient), polyuria accompanied by a dilute urine (urine- specific gravity is low, less than 1.003), increased serum osmolality, and hypernatremia. Patients who are awake and can maintain oral input rarely become hypernatremic. However, this is not the case for those with impaired mentation. Definitive treatment of DI includes desmopressin (DDAVP) (10–20 mcg intranasal, 2–4 mcg subcutaneously [SC]) or vasopressin (5 U every 2–4 hours intramuscularly or SC). Care should be taken when using desmopressin because hyponatremia can ensue.<sup>161</sup> Patients with nephrogenic DI can be treated with chlorpropamide.

### Intraoperative

Maintaining intravascular volume and correcting the sodium concentrations are goals. Normal saline (NS) is used to restore volume and correct hypernatremia for severe situations. Fluids include hourly maintenance as well as twothirds of the urine output in the previous hour. Hypotonic fluids containing 0.45% NS are commonly used as maintenance fluids in order to replace water deficits.

### Postoperative

Patients should be asked about thirst and fluid intake. Output is assessed and recorded, and electrolytes monitored. Replacement of previous and ongoing fluid losses is important. Hypernatremia can occur if thirst is impaired, or the patient has no access to water. Patients with partial diabetes insipidus and mild to moderate polyuria may be adequately treated with a low- solute diet or a thiazide diuretic. Desmopressin is often required especially for those with severe diabetes insipidus. Patients may be accustomed to drinking large amounts of fluids, and along with desmopressin may be at risk of hyponatremia.

### SYNDROME OF INAPPROPRIATE ANTIDIURETIC HORMONE (SIADH)

Inappropriate secretion of vasopressin, without relation to serum osmolality, results in hyponatremia and fluid retention. This inappropriate secretion can result from a variety



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 32 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 32 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0032.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 33 -->

of CNS lesions, drugs including antidepressants, pulmonary infections, hypothyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, and ectopic production from tumors. The diagnosis of SIADH should be suspected in any patient with hyponatremia who excretes urine that is hypertonic relative to plasma. The following laboratory findings further support the diagnosis:

- Urinary sodium greater than 20 mEq/L

- Low serum concentrations of blood urea nitrogen (BUN), creatinine, uric acid, and albumin

- Serum sodium less than 130 mEq/L

- Plasma osmolality less than 270 mOsm/L

- Urine hypertonic relative to plasma

Most of the clinical features associated with SIADH are related to hyponatremia and the resulting brain edema. Features include weight gain, weakness, lethargy, mental confusion, obtundation, and disordered reflexes, and may culminate in convulsions and coma.

### Preoperative

Treatment of SIADH relies on appropriate treatment of the causative disorders (e.g., drug withdrawal if a certain medication is responsible) and restriction of water. Occasionally, drugs that inhibit the renal response to ADH (e.g., lithium or demeclocycline) should be administered preoperatively to restore normal intravascular volume and electrolytes. Lithium is rarely used due to toxicity but demeclocycline in doses of 900 to 1200 mg/day interferes with the ability of the renal tubules to concentrate urine, thereby causing excretion of isotonic or hypotonic urine and lessening the hyponatremia. Fluid restriction is the other key component of therapy, keeping in mind that too vigorous treatment of chronic hyponatremia can result in disabling osmotic demyelination syndrome, or central pontine myelinolysis. The increase in serum sodium concentrations should not be greater than 8 mEq/L in 24 hours. Patients with mild to moderate symptoms of water intoxication can be treated with restriction of fluid intake to approximately 500 to 1000 mL/day. Patients with severe water intoxication and CNS symptoms may need vigorous treatment consisting of intravenous administration of hypertonic saline solutions until symptoms resolve, followed by fluid restriction.

### Intraoperative

Attention should be paid to volume status. However, these patients are generally euvolemic or mildly hypervolemic. Invasive monitoring is rarely necessary and is dictated by the underlying pathology and the proposed procedure rather than the presence of SIADH. The type of anesthetic used has no bearing on the postoperative development of SIADH.

### Postoperative

Fluid restriction is a mainstay of therapy in most patients with SIADH, with a suggested goal intake of less than 800 mL/day, together with treatment of infections such as pneumonia, and cessation of offending drugs, such as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors or chlorpropamide.

### CARCINOID SYNDROME

Carcinoid tumors are neuroendocrine neoplasms arising from the appendix, small intestine, rectum, or bronchi.

Carcinoid tumors contain several peptides that may or may not be secreted in the systemic circulation. Even when secreted from the GI tract, these peptides are inactivated by the liver. Patients with carcinoid tumors present with symptoms only when liver metastases are present or there is a bronchial neoplasm, and the liver metabolism is bypassed. Symptoms of carcinoid syndrome are the result of circulating neuropeptides (e.g., bradykinin, kallikrein, substance P, dopamine, norepinephrine, histamine) but mostly serotonin or its metabolite, 5- hydroxyindoleacetic acid (5HIAA). Manifestations of carcinoid syndrome include flushing (usually has rapid onset), diarrhea, bronchospasm, and hemodynamic instability (hypo- or hypertension). Patients with carcinoid syndrome can have cardiac involvement manifested as right- sided valvular lesions (tricuspid or pulmonary valve stenosis or regurgitation) due to fibrosis precipitated by the circulating vasoactive substances. Because the pulmonary circulation deactivates serotonin and other active peptides, left- sided valve lesions are rare (10% of patients) and only present with bronchial carcinoids or when there is a right to left intracardiac shunt. Carcinoid crisis is a severe form of carcinoid syndrome manifested by hemodynamic instability, intense flushing, diarrhea, and bronchoconstriction.

Diagnosis of carcinoid syndrome is made clinically and confirmed by high serum serotonin or serum or urine 5-HIAA concentrations. Chromogranin A concentrations are elevated in carcinoid syndrome but lack specificity. Chromogranin A concentrations are usually used to assess disease recurrence or progression rather than for diagnostic purposes.

### Preoperative

Optimization focuses on control of the carcinoid symptoms. As carcinoids are slow- growing tumors, symptom control should be achieved preoperatively in order to avoid an intraoperative carcinoid crisis. Medical treatment with long- acting somatostatin analogues started in the outpatient setting should be continued preoperatively, with the dose adjusted based on symptoms and on urinary concentrations of 5- HIAA. Lanreotide is a long- acting somatostatin analogue, given as a depot intramuscularly every month. As mentioned, patients with carcinoid syndrome can present with right- sided valvular lesions (due to fibrin deposits) or even progression to right ventricular failure.<sup>162</sup> An echocardiogram should be performed in symptomatic patients.<sup>163</sup> Electrolyte disturbances and malnutrition caused by diarrhea should be addressed before surgery.

### Intraoperative

Balanced general anesthesia is the anesthetic technique of choice in patients with carcinoids. Anesthetic agents that do not promote histamine release are safe. Preoperative benzodiazepines are useful in decreasing anxiety and an arterial line is recommended due to possible blood pressure fluctuations. Use of a TEE should be considered if cardiac involvement is suspected. Although short- acting octreotide prophylaxis is not routinely recommended, some centers use subcutaneous or intravenous octreotide administered 6 to 24 hours before major surgeries. While this may not avoid a crisis, it minimizes the severity of the symptoms.<sup>164</sup> Octreotide administration for minor procedures is not routinely

<!-- PDF page 34 -->

used, but special attention during the intraoperative and postoperative phases is required since a crisis can develop. A preoperative bolus of 500 mcg octreotide followed by an intraoperative infusion at a rate of 500 mcg/h prevented 70% of patients with carcinoid tumors from developing carcinoid crisis and appears safe.<sup>165</sup> Intraoperatively, a rapid increase in airway pressures suggestive of bronchospasm might prompt a diagnosis of a carcinoid crisis. Despite earlier concerns, β- adrenergic agonist administration may not precipitate carcinoid crisis, as recently evidenced.<sup>166</sup> Vasopressors and antihypertensive agents should be readily available to treat blood pressure fluctuations. However, octreotide boluses are the modality of choice to treat hypotension and bronchoconstriction. Hyperglycemia due to octreotide is common and may require an insulin infusion.<sup>167</sup>

### Postoperative

Patients should be admitted to the ICU and monitored for subsequent carcinoid crises. Octreotide as described in the intraoperative management is used if needed.

### HYPOKALEMIA

Hypokalemia is defined as a serum potassium (K) concentration of less than 3.5 mEq/L and can be acute (usually due to intracellular movement of potassium, such as during metabolic alkalosis) or chronic (due to gastrointestinal or renal losses) (Table 29.14). Patients with mild, chronic hypokalemia (K 3–3.5 mEq/L) do not present with any symptoms. However, those with severe, acute hypokalemia (K less than 3 mEq/L) can have muscle weakness, depressed deep tendon reflexes, palpitations, arrhythmias, and polyuria due to inability to concentrate urine. Severe

**Table 29.14** Potential Causes of Hypokalemia

|**Potassium Losses**|**Inadequate Intake**|**Intracellular K Shift**|
|---|---|---|
|**GASTROINTESTINAL**|Anorexia|Alkalemia|
|Diarrhea<br>Fistula<br>Ileostomy|Starvation<br>Total parenteral<br>nutrition|Hypothermia<br>Barium toxicity<br>Insulin<br>|
|Laxative abuse<br>**RENAL**||Beta- adrenergic<br>agonists|
|Hypomagnesemia<br>Hyperglycemia<br>Salt wasting<br>nephropathy<br>(Bartter syndrome)|||
|Renal tubular acidosis|||
|Mineralocorticoid<br>excess (Conn<br>syndrome)|||
|Drug induced|||
|(amphotericin B,<br>aminoglycosides,<br>cisplatin, loop<br>diuretics, high-dose<br>penicillin)|||
|Dialysis|||
|Plasmapheresis|||
|**CUTANEOUS**|||
|Burns|||
|Diaphoresis|||


hypokalemia can manifest with ECG changes such as flattening of T waves, QT prolongation, U waves, and ST depression.

Patients with mild hypokalemia, whether it is acute or chronic, can proceed to surgery, but need further evaluation and treatment. Care should be taken to avoid further decreases in potassium, which can trigger cardiac instability. Alkalosis will lower potassium concentrations. For patients with severe hypokalemia, the approach is a matter of debate and controversy. Potassium repletion is considered for acute hypokalemia. The underlying cause of chronic hypokalemia such as gastrointestinal or renal losses should be addressed. Potassium repletion in the setting of chronic total-body potassium depletion, such as in chronic loss scenarios, may require considerable doses and there is concern that acutely raising potassium concentrations may promote cardiac instability rather than stabilizing the membranes. Potassium repletion should be administered in small doses of 20–40 mEq over 30 minutes or 3–10 minutes when lifethreatening arrhythmias are present. Magnesium repletion is added even when magnesium concentrations are normal but more so when low, to ensure maintenance of intracellular potassium concentrations. In hypokalemic patients taking digoxin, the electrolyte abnormality can precipitate toxicity.

### Intraoperative

Potassium concentrations do not impact anesthetic management (potassium does not affect the minimal alveolar concentration of inhalational anesthetics). Care is taken to avoid further decreases in potassium concentrations by avoiding fluctuating acid–base status or administration of certain medications such as the antibiotic gentamicin or insulin.

### Postoperative

Monitoring and replacing deficits are important.

### HYPERKALEMIA

Hyperkalemia is defined as a potassium concentration of greater than 5 mEq/L, and can be the result of medication side effects, diet, and chronic kidney disease (Table 29.15). Hyperkalemia can be mild (5–6 mEq/L without ECG changes), moderate (5–6 mEq/L with ECG changes or 6.1–6.5 mEq/L without ECG changes), or severe (6.1– 6.5 mEq/L with ECG changes) or greater than 6.5 mEq/L. Patients with mild to moderate hyperkalemia are usually asymptomatic but those with severely elevated potassium concentrations can have muscle cramps, tetany, dyspnea due to ventilatory muscle weakness, paresthesia, lethargy, bradycardia, irregular heart rhythm, and ECG changes. ECG changes include peaked symmetric T waves, shortened PR interval, ST depressions, and widened QRS complex. The “sine wave” is a sign of impending cardiac arrest and is often a fatal rhythm.

For patients with mild to moderate acute or chronic hyperkalemia, treatment relies on eliminating potassium from the body with a GI ion-binding and exchange resin and loop diuretics. Patients with severe hyperkalemia (or moderate but with ECG changes) should be given calcium (as a membrane stabilizer), drugs to redistribute potassium

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 34 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0034.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 35 -->

**Table 29.15** Differential Diagnosis of Hyperkalemia

|**Mechanism**|**Diagnosis**|
|---|---|
|Miscellaneous|Spurious (pseudohyperkalemia)<br>Hemolysis<br>Thrombocytosis<br>Leukemia|
|Impaired potassium excretion|Renal failure<br>Drug effect (ACEIs, ARBs, potassium<br>sparing diuretics, NSAIDs, direct<br>renin inhibitors)<br>Mineralocorticoid deficiency<br>(Addison disease)|
|Extracellular potassium shift|Hyperglycemia<br>Insulin deficiency<br>Rhabdomyolysis<br>Trauma<br>Drug effect – beta blockers,<br>succinylcholine, digoxin,<br>calcineurin inhibitors|

_ACEIs_ , angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors; _ARBs_ , angiotensin receptor blockers; _NSAIDs_ , nonsteroidal anti- inflammatory drugs.

from the extracellular to the intracellular space (insulin, β- agonists, bicarbonate), and have potassium removed from the body, as discussed previously. For patients with end- stage kidney disease, dialysis may be necessary as they may not be able to eliminate potassium through the urine.

### Intraoperative

Perioperative management consists of maintaining a safe potassium concentration (less than 5.5 mEq/L) for patients undergoing elective surgery. The activity of NMBA may be potentiated and perioperative care should prevent further increases in potassium by avoiding acidosis and drugs such as succinylcholine. It is important to note that succinylcholine only increases the potassium concentration by 0.5 mEq/L both in patients with and without kidney failure.

### Postoperative

All of the issues and recommendations discussed in the preoperative management of hyperkalemia should be applied in the postoperative period.

## Rheumatic and Connective Tissue Diseases

Patients with rheumatic or connective tissue diseases frequently have dysfunction of several organ systems. A detailed history and review of systems that focus on potential manifestations of disease are essential in perioperative planning. Diagnostic laboratory testing is indicated to determine the stability of end-organ function (particularly hepatic and renal function); the presence of anemia of chronic disease and concomitant iron deficiency anemia; the presence of malnutrition; or coagulation disturbances due to myelosuppression or liver disease.

In addition to routine cardiac risk assessment (see Chapter 28), non- traditional risk factors are considered

in assessing for risk of ischemic heart disease. Inflammatory diseases and chronic corticosteroid use can cause accelerated atherosclerosis in the absence of advanced age or traditional cardiovascular risk factors. Rheumatic and connective tissue diseases can be associated with other cardiovascular manifestations, such as conduction disturbances, myocarditis, or pericardial disease; focused assessment for these issues is warranted if clinical suspicion exists.

Patients with rheumatic diseases, such as systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), rheumatoid arthritis (RA), ankylosing spondylitis, or systemic sclerosis frequently suffer from chronic daily pain. Many will use antispasmodics, antidepressants, anticonvulsants, nonsteroidal anti- inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), or opioids for pain control.<sup>168</sup> Patients taking opioids chronically are more at risk for postoperative complications after major surgery; they have higher rates of readmission to hospital after major surgery, longer lengths of stay, higher hospital costs, and are more likely to be readmitted for infection, opioid overdose, or acute pain.<sup>169</sup> For patients on chronic opioid therapy, challenging postoperative pain management is anticipated and multimodal analgesia is employed, including continuation of home pain medications. Regional analgesia is employed whenever feasible to reduce opioid requirements.

Specific aspects unique to rheumatic and connective tissue diseases with relevance for perioperative management are highlighted below.

### SYSTEMIC LUPUS ERYTHEMATOSUS (SLE)

### Preoperative

SLE is a multisystem inflammatory disease. While mortality rates from SLE have dramatically decreased from the 1940s when expected 3- year mortality was 40%, observed yearly mortality from SLE is still two to five times higher than for the general population.<sup>170</sup> Total hip and knee arthroplasty are common surgical procedures in patients with SLE. Historically, the primary pathology driving need for early joint replacement was osteonecrosis, yet now, patients are increasingly presenting at an older age with osteoarthritis, indicating patients with SLE are surviving to develop degenerative disease.<sup>171</sup> Preoperative assessment focuses on eliciting manifestations of disease and determining the stability of organ function. Specific organ system manifestations along with potential perioperative implications are listed in Table 29.16.

Consequences from medical treatment of SLE include recurrent infections from immunosuppression; adrenal suppression from chronic steroid use; hepatotoxicity from disease- modifying antirheumatic drugs (DMARDs); or coagulation disturbances from aspirin therapy, NSAID use, or anticoagulants. The 2017 American College of Rheumatology (ACR)/American Association of Hip and Knee Surgeons Guideline for the Perioperative Management of Antirheumatic Medication in Patients with Rheumatic Diseases Undergoing Elective Total Hip or Total Knee Arthroplasty<sup>172</sup> provides perioperative medication recommendations for patients with severe and not- severe SLE who are otherwise optimized for surgery (Table 29.17). Severe SLE is defined in the guideline as<sup>172</sup> :

|**Pulmonary**|ᑏPleural disease: pleuritic chest pain, pleural effusion<br>ᑏInterstitial lung disease, restrictive lung disease, and hypoxia<br>ᑏPulmonary hypertension<br>ᑏPulmonary hemorrhage|ᑏRisk for perioperative pulmonary complications<br>ᑏOxygen desaturation perioperatively|
|**Renal**|ᑏGlomerulonephritis, chronic kidney disease progressing to<br>end- stage renal disease|ᑏAvoid nephrotoxins/IV contrast<br>ᑏPerioperative fluid and blood pressure management to<br>prevent acute kidney injury|
|**Hematologic**|ᑏAnemia: of chronic disease, iron deficiency, or hemolytic<br>ᑏLeukopenia<br>ᑏThrombocytopenia, immune thrombocytopenia (ITP)<br>ᑏAntiphospholipid antibodies (“lupus anticoagulant”)|ᑏIncreased risk of blood transfusions<br>ᑏOccult iron deficiency anemia<br>ᑏElevated aPTT with thromboembolic risk|
|**Musculoskeletal**|ᑏArthritis, arthralgia, myalgia, osteonecrosis|ᑏChronic opioid therapy and challenging perioperative pain<br>management|
|**Integumentary/**<br>**mucous**<br>**membranes**|ᑏDermatitis<br>ᑏOral or nasal ulcers|ᑏSkin sensitivity to tapes/adhesives<br>ᑏEpistaxis with nasal instrumentation|


_aPTT_ , activated partial thromboplas tin time; _IV_ , intravenous.

_Currently treated (induction or maintenance) for severe organ manifestations: lupus nephritis, central nervous system lupus, severe hemolytic anemia (hemoglobin &lt;9.9), platelets &lt;50,000/ml, vasculitis (other than mild cutaneous vasculitis), including pulmonary hemorrhage, myocarditis, lupus pneumonitis, severe myositis (with muscle weakness, not just high enzymes), lupus enteritis (vasculitis), lupus pancreatitis, cholecystitis, lupus hepatitis, protein- losing enteropathy, malabsorption, orbital inflammation/myositis, severe keratitis, posterior severe uveitis/retinal vasculitis, severe scleritis, optic neuritis, anterior ischemic optic neuropathy_ …

If the patient with SLE is to undergo more than minor surgery, appropriate preoperative testing includes a complete blood count, serum creatinine and electrolytes, iron studies (ferritin and transferrin saturation), and blood type and screen (T&S) if anemia is present. If occult cardiac disease or myocarditis is suspected (e.g., presence of a heart murmur, significant dyspnea on exertion, resting sinus tachycardia, conduction abnormalities, signs of volume overload), workup may include assessment for ischemic heart disease, heart failure, or heart rhythm abnormalities.

### Intraoperative

Patients with SLE are commonly young females, yet they are at increased risk for perioperative major adverse cardiac events (MACE),<sup>173</sup> acute kidney injury (AKI),<sup>174</sup> and

stroke.<sup>175</sup> Avoiding perioperative hypotension is critical to preventing these adverse outcomes, with a mean arterial pressure of 65–70 mm Hg likely sufficient for most patients.<sup>1</sup> For patients chronically taking corticosteroids, an additional intraoperative “stress” dose may be warranted for major surgery, particularly in the setting of intraoperative hypotension (see earlier section on Adrenal Insufficiency).

### Postoperative

Chronic pain is a frequent manifestation of disease,<sup>176</sup> with the yearly prevalence of opioid use of 53% in all SLE patients and with 18% of patients using opioids chronically.<sup>168</sup> In addition to contributing to challenging postoperative pain management, chronic opioid use increases perioperative risk of complications.

### RHEUMATOID ARTHRITIS (RA)

### Preoperative

RA is a polyarticular, inflammatory arthritis that causes joint destruction and extra- articular manifestations. Preoperative assessment focuses on disease severity, duration of symptoms, and current medical therapy. See Table 29.18 for a list of potential disease involvement by organ system. In the nonsurgical population, patients with RA experience excess cardiovascular mortality, with an increased risk of cardiovascular events and cardiovascular disease–related
