endotracheal tube may help to avoid laryngeal trauma, or avoiding intubation entirely may be preferable and using a supraglottic airway (SGA) if otherwise appropriate.

### Postoperative

While very rare, case reports exist documenting acute postoperative airway obstruction requiring emergency surgical airway management due to vocal cord abnormalities attributed to cricoarytenoid arthritis.<sup>181</sup> Close monitoring for airway compromise is warranted after extubation in patients with preoperative symptoms or vocal cord abnormalities.

Patients with long- standing, severe disease may develop anterior subluxation of the atlanto- axial joint which can be asymptomatic, or cause pain radiating to the occiput, myelopathy, or sensory disturbance in the C2 distribution. With the advent of more effective DMARDs, the prevalence of anterior atlantoaxial subluxation reported in the literature has decreased from 36% prior to the 1980s to 24% of patients in the 2000s.<sup>178</sup> Plain lateral radiographs of the cervical spine in flexion and extension positions may reveal abnormal separation of the odontoid process and the C1 arch, however, preoperative radiographs are frequently of inadequate quality to provide diagnostic value.<sup>179</sup> Hoarseness and stridor on exam are potential signs of cricoarytenoid arthritis, a relatively common finding in patients with severe long-standing RA (30%–50% of patients as detected on indirect laryngoscopy or CT).<sup>180</sup>

Opioid use is common in patients with rheumatoid arthritis, with a quarter of outpatient clinic visits for RA resulting in a prescription for opioids in the United States.<sup>168</sup> In addition to contributing to challenging postoperative pain management, chronic opioid use increases perioperative risk.

### ANKYLOSING SPONDYLITIS

### Preoperative

Ankylosing spondylitis (axial spondylarthritis) is an inflammatory arthritis of the spine causing progressive spinal fusion and impaired spinal mobility. The term “bamboo spine” is used to describe the pathognomonic radiographic findings in advanced disease. Patients with bamboo spine often have a stooped posture resulting from flexion deformities at the neck and hips, hyperkyphosis of the thoracic spine, and loss of lumbar spine lordosis. Spinal rigidity puts patients at high risk for spinal fractures after even minor trauma, most commonly (80%) in the cervical spine at C5–C6 or C6–C7.<sup>182</sup> Reports exist of devastating neurologic injury resulting from inadvertent hyperextension of the neck with direct laryngoscopy<sup>183</sup> and videolaryngoscopy<sup>184</sup> during difficult intubation. Anterior atlantoaxial subluxation occurs frequently (14% of patients in an outpatient ankylosing spondylitis clinic),<sup>185</sup> though may be asymptomatic. Careful physical examination for spinal abnormalities and review of relevant imaging is essential in anesthetic planning.

Early treatment with DMARDs is advocated in RA to prevent joint destruction. Methotrexate is commonly used for maintenance, with NSAIDs and glucocorticoids used for initial symptom control and then rapidly weaned. Potential toxicity with low- dose methotrexate used for RA includes hepatotoxicity (steatosis, fibrosis, cirrhosis), pulmonary toxicity (pneumonitis, fibrosis, pulmonary infections), and myelosuppression (macrocytosis, pancytopenia). See Table 29.17 for perioperative medication management recommendations.

### Intraoperative

If general anesthesia is planned, airway management considers the potential for occult atlantoaxial subluxation and cervical spine arthritis. Minimizing neck movement after induction of anesthesia can be achieved through video laryngoscopy or fiberoptic bronchoscopic intubation combined with manual in- line stabilization of the neck. In the setting of cricoarytenoid arthritis, a smaller-sized

Patients with long- standing ankylosing spondylitis may present with non- articular organ system dysfunction. See Table 29.19 for a summary of issues relevant to anesthesia. Restrictive lung disease occurs in long- standing disease due to thoracic cage immobility and poor chest wall compliance. Unlike in RA, cardiovascular risk in ankylosing spondylitis is only mildly increased over the general population.<sup>186</sup> Systemic sclerosis can cause aortic root and aortic valve abnormalities. Renal disease may be present.

<!-- PDF page 39 -->

NSAIDs are the recommended first- line treatment for symptom management, with many patients taking chronic NSAIDs long term. Non- biologic and biologic DMARDs are used to prevent progression of spinal disease and maintain function. Unlike in RA, oral glucocorticoids are not used. See Table 29.17 for perioperative medication management.

### Intraoperative

The presence of spinal rigidity (bamboo spine) poses challenges for intraoperative positioning and airway management, and complicates attempts at neuraxial anesthesia. Patients with spinal deformities may be unable to lie flat. Given the propensity for spinal fractures, padding is used to support the shoulders and neck to avoid hyperextension. A semi- sitting position helps accommodate flexion deformities of the hips.

Predictors of difficult intubation (reduced neck extension, reduced inter- incisor distance, and higher Mallampati score) are present more frequently in patients with severe spinal disease.<sup>187</sup> Patients found to have a Cormack and Lehane grade III or IV glottis with direct laryngoscopy can usually be successfully intubated with video laryngoscopy, yet failures with this technique also occur.<sup>184,188</sup> The use of flexible fiberoptic bronchoscopy, with the patient either awake or asleep, may be the preferred intubation technique in patients with predicted difficult intubation and severe cervical spine disease.

An attempt at neuraxial anesthesia can be offered to patients, with the caveat that there is a higher rate of unsuccessful placement of spinal anesthesia.<sup>189</sup>

### Postoperative

Opioid use is common in ankylosing spondylitis, with 36% of patients using opioids even when optimized on biologic agents for their disease.<sup>168</sup> In addition to contributing to challenging postoperative pain management, chronic opioid use contributes to increased perioperative risk.

is aimed at specific organ system involvement, with some patients also taking immunosuppressants (e.g., mycophenolate, cyclophosphamide, low- dose glucocorticoids).

### Intraoperative

Specific intraoperative management is dictated by the disease sequelae present (see Table 29.20). Difficult intravenous access is common due to skin thickening.<sup>190</sup> Difficult airway management is anticipated if fibrotic changes of the mouth, temporomandibular joint, and cervical spine are present. Patients with PAH are at high risk intraoperatively and postoperatively for hemodynamic compromise from right heart failure. See earlier section on Pulmonary Hypertension for further information on intraoperative management. Patients with esophageal disease are at risk for aspiration at induction and pharmacologic prophylaxis can be considered.

High- dose glucocorticoid administration can precipitate SRC. Although the condition is uncommon, a position paper from a multidisciplinary working group in France recommends keeping dosages of prednisone to less than 15 mg/ day (approximately 2.5 mg of dexamethasone), so alternative strategies for postoperative nausea and vomiting prophylaxis should be employed.<sup>192</sup>

### Postoperative

Following major surgery requiring hospital admission, postoperative cardiac telemetry and pulse oximetry monitoring may be appropriate in patients with dysrhythmias, risk of myocardial ischemia, risk for hypoxia with ILD, or refractory hypertension. Admission to the intensive care unit (ICU) with invasive hemodynamic monitoring may be appropriate for patients with pulmonary hypertension or heart failure. In patients with a known difficult airway and ILD, careful monitoring for early signs of deterioration and an established plan for reintubation may help mitigate the risk of urgent airway intervention.

### SYSTEMIC SCLEROSIS (SCLERODERMA)

### Preoperative

Commonly known as scleroderma, the immune- mediated disease systemic sclerosis is characterized by thickening and hardening of the skin alongside multiorgan system dysfunction. Patients are often young at the time of diagnosis, yet develop severely disabling and life- threatening disease manifestations, including perioral fibrosis and joint contractures; interstitial lung disease (ILD) and pulmonary artery hypertension (PAH); heart failure; myocardial ischemia; and malignant hypertension from scleroderma renal crisis (SRC). In the non- perioperative setting, the high risk of mortality is reflected by an age- adjusted death rate 3.5 times that of the general population.<sup>190</sup> Mortality is most often related to cardiac complications, even in the absence of traditional risk factors for atherosclerosis, followed by pulmonary complications.<sup>190</sup> Traditional perioperative cardiac risk calculators may underestimate risk, though true perioperative risk is unclear,<sup>191</sup> given the rarity of disease and the wide spectrum of clinical presentation.

See Table 29.20 for clinical features of systemic sclerosis and potential perioperative considerations. Preoperative evaluation focuses on determining the presence and severity of organ dysfunction. Since the underlying pathogenesis of systemic sclerosis is poorly understood, medical treatment

### MARFAN SYNDROME

### Preoperative

Marfan syndrome is an inherited connective tissue disorder with variable presentation and severity in individuals. Preoperative assessment focuses on identifying clinical manifestations with relevance to anesthesia (Box 29.11). Patients with aortic root dilation are treated with longacting beta blockers, which are continued perioperatively.

### Intraoperative

For general anesthesia, the intubation technique considers laxity in the temporomandibular joint and potential dislocation with jaw thrust and laryngoscopy. Minimizing neck extension is advisable in the presence of cervical spine ligamentous laxity. Peak airway pressures are minimized in patients at risk for spontaneous pneumothorax. Hemodynamic goals for patients with aortic root dilation are established to avoid hypertension and tachycardia, which could precipitate an aortic dissection. Difficulty is anticipated with neuraxial anesthesia in the presence of significant kyphoscoliosis.

### Postoperative

Blood pressure should be controlled with similar parameters to intraoperative management.

### EHLERS–DANLOS SYNDROME (EDS)

### Preoperative

Ehlers–Danlos syndrome (EDS) refers to a group of genetic connective tissue disorders with common characteristics including joint hypermobility, tissue fragility, and skin hyperextensibility in varying degrees. A new international classification system published in 2017 defined 13 types based on common clinical features.<sup>193</sup> Preoperative assessment focuses on identifying features relevant to anesthetic management based on the type (Table 29.21).<sup>193</sup> The clinical features present in Type 4 (vascular EDS) have the greatest potential risk of perioperative mortality (spontaneous arterial or uterine rupture), however, all types have significant potential for complications. Despite a clinical bleeding history, standard coagulation tests such as prothrombin time/international normalized ratio (PT/INR), activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT), and thromboelastometry are frequently normal. Platelet aggregation tests are abnormal in one quarter of patients.<sup>194</sup>

### Intraoperative

Patients with joint hypermobility require careful positioning to prevent injury. Additional caution with eye protection and padding is warranted in EDS types with eye fragility. Friable skin that easily bruises is carefully

padded and strong adhesive tapes are avoided whenever possible. There is high risk of bleeding with vascular access or regional anesthesia, and risk of vessel wall dissection with arterial line placement. Repeated blood pressure cuff inflation can cause skin trauma, so the risks and benefits of invasive versus noninvasive blood pressure monitoring are weighed. Pneumatic tourniquet application is avoided unless absolutely necessary.

Precautions with intubation include avoiding temporomandibular joint subluxation, avoiding repeated intubation attempts, and a smaller-sized endotracheal tube to avoid mucosal trauma. Ventilation with low peak airway pressures may help avoid pneumothorax.

Preparations for excess intraoperative bleeding include cell- saver and blood cross- matching. Desmopressin (DDAVP) has shown benefit in EDS- associated bleeding, though the exact mechanism is unknown.<sup>194</sup>

In Type 4 (vascular) EDS, authors have recommended against performing neuraxial anesthesia due to increased risk of epidural hematoma, though clear evidence delineating risks does not exist.<sup>194</sup> Neuraxial anesthesia can be offered in other EDS types, though may be complicated by spine pathology.

### Postoperative

Patients with Type 4 (vascular) EDS are at highest risk for mortality and excessive bleeding postoperatively and even minor surgery carries increased risk.<sup>195</sup> Spontaneous hollow viscous rupture occurs most commonly in Type 4 EDS, though it can occur in any type, so adequate prophylaxis for postoperative nausea and vomiting is advised.

### ACHONDROPLASIA

### Preoperative

Achondroplasia is a type of skeletal dysplasia that results in short stature, long- bone shortening, and macrocephaly.

|1|Classical EDS|_Major:_skin hyperextensibility, joint hypermobility<br>_Minor_: easy bruising, skin fragility|
|2|Classical- like EDS|_Major:_skin hyperextensibility, joint hypermobility, spontaneous skin bruising<br>_Minor:_leg edema, polyneuropathy|

|10|Spondylodysplastic EDS|_Major:_short stature, muscle hypotonia<br>_Minor:_skin hyperextensibility, delayed motor and cognitive development|

|13|Periodontal EDS|_Major:_early onset, severe periodontitis<br>_Minor:_easy bruising, joint hypermobility, skin hyperextensibility, prominent vasculature|


Anatomic abnormalities can lead to cardiorespiratory and neurologic conditions with important relevance to anesthesia. Preoperative evaluation includes assessing for undiagnosed sleep- disordered breathing (both obstructive and central sleep apnea); restrictive pulmonary disease from thoracic cage abnormalities; decreased functional capacity and obesity; cervical spinal stenosis or atlantoaxial subluxation; and upper airway abnormalities. Patients with chronic hypoxia, untreated sleep apnea, upper airway obstruction, restrictive pulmonary disease, or hypoventilation are at risk for pulmonary hypertension. See section on Pulmonary Hypertension for more details.

Perioperative risk of morbidity and mortality is elevated for patients with achondroplasia. A multispecialty group has published best practices for perioperative care in patients with skeletal dysplasia.<sup>196</sup>

### Intraoperative

Difficult airway management is anticipated based on a short neck, decreased neck extension, and large tongue relative to a small trachea. For safe airway management, selecting a smaller-sized endotracheal tube than anticipated for age and minimizing neck extension with videolaryngoscopy or fiberoptic bronchoscopic intubation is appropriate.

Challenging neuraxial anesthesia is anticipated due to kyphoscoliosis, a narrow epidural space, and vertebral body abnormalities. A reduced dose of local anesthetic for

spinal anesthesia is anticipated, yet the optimal adjustment is unknown. Doses of spinal hyperbaric bupivacaine administered for cesarean section in case reports range from 6.75 mg to 10 mg.<sup>197</sup> Thoracolumbar spinal stenosis is common and makes local anesthetic spread unpredictable and potentially increases the risk of neurologic complications.<sup>198</sup>

### Postoperative

Extubation should occur in a location with difficult airway equipment readily available, in the event of urgent reintubation. After major surgery, postoperative monitoring in the ICU is appropriate for patients with severe pulmonary hypertension, worsening of respiratory symptoms, or concern for hypoventilation with opioids.

## Infectious Diseases

### HUMAN IMMUNODEFICIENCY VIRUS (HIV)/ ACQUIRED IMMUNODEFICIENCY SYNDROME (AIDS)

### Preoperative

HIV infection is now treated as a chronic disease, with modern antiretroviral therapy (ART) effectively suppressing progression of disease for decades. Patients are defined as



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 41 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 41 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0041.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 42 -->

having AIDS if their CD4 count falls below 200 cells/mcL, or if they develop an AIDS- defining illness (e.g., candidiasis of esophagus, cytomegalovirus disease, HIV- related encephalopathy, disseminated histoplasmosis, Kaposi sarcoma, primary brain lymphoma, pneumocystis pneumonia, toxoplasmosis of the brain, wasting syndrome). Chronic HIV infection leads to multisystem abnormalities from chronic inflammation and medication toxicities. See Table 29.22 for potential organ system manifestations occurring during chronic HIV infection, or ones that are mainly seen after an AIDS diagnosis. A retrospective Medicare database study of patients who underwent elective total shoulder arthroplasty illustrate the broad array of risk factors present in HIV- positive patients, who had a higher prevalence of almost every comorbidity examined.<sup>199</sup> Compared with the control group, HIV- positive patients were more likely to have HF (odds ratio [OR] 3.09 [2.73–3.50]), valvular disease (OR 3.33 [2.95–3.76]), peripheral vascular disease (OR 3.50 [3.11–3.93]), hypertension (OR 4.23 [3.59– 4.97]), paralysis (OR 19.09 [16.42–22.20]), other neurologic disorder (OR 5.70 [5.07–6.40]), chronic pulmonary disease (OR 2.34 [2.10–2.62]), diabetes with chronic complications (OR 2.85 [2.41–3.37]), renal failure (OR 2.86 [2.47–3.30]), liver disease (OR 2.40 [1.89–3.04]), coagulation deficiency (OR 3.57 [3.05–4.20]), blood loss anemia (OR 2.60 [2.08–3.24]), fluid and electrolyte disorder (OR 2.97 [2.66–3.31]), alcohol abuse (OR 2.86 [2.22–3.70]), and psychoses (OR 4.13 [OR 3.55–4.82]).<sup>199</sup> See Chapter 28 for further details on the assessment of patients with acute HIV infection.

Preoperative evaluation focuses on assessing for stability of organ function and adequacy of ART therapy. Measurement of HIV viral load within 6 months and CD4 count within 1 year in an otherwise stable patient is sufficient. A patient taking ART with an HIV viral load &lt;200 copies/mL and CD4 count >200 cells/mm<sup>3</sup> has a similar perioperative risk to someone without HIV and may proceed with elective surgery.<sup>200</sup> Patients with low CD4 count and uncontrolled HIV viral load are at increased risk of death, infectious and noninfectious complications, and prolonged length of stay.<sup>201</sup> In patients with poorly controlled disease, consultation with an HIV care provider will determine if further optimization of ART therapy will improve clinical status. If

no change to ART is advised, patients are counseled on the increased perioperative risks in the setting of uncontrolled HIV. All ART therapy is continued perioperatively.

### Intraoperative

Important drug–drug interactions between ART and perioperative medications are possible. Patients may be treated with boosted protease inhibitor (boosted PI) monotherapy as a simpler alternative to three- drug ART regimens. All boosted PIs strongly inhibit CYP34A, causing increased blood levels of fentanyl, lidocaine, rocuronium, and midazolam; dose adjustment and monitoring for adverse effects is recommended.<sup>201</sup> Reduced drug absorption is anticipated with proton pump inhibitor or histamine- 2 receptor agonist with some ART regimens; use is contraindicated or avoided when possible.<sup>200</sup> No drug interactions are anticipated with propofol, sevoflurane, succinylcholine or sugammadex, and ART. A free drug interaction tool is available at www.hiv- druginteractions.org maintained by the University of Liverpool.

### Postoperative

HIV infection creates a hypercoagulable state, with patients at increased risk of thromboembolic disease (both deep venous thrombosis [DVT] and pulmonary embolism) compared with age- adjusted HIV- negative patients.<sup>202</sup> Early initiation of pharmacologic DVT prophylaxis and mobilization is recommended.

### CORONAVIRUS DISEASE 2019 (COVID- 19)

### Preoperative

The emergence of the novel SARS- CoV- 2 causing coronavirus disease in late 2019 (COVID- 19), and subsequent worldwide pandemic, created an evolving challenge in perioperative patient management. Early in the pandemic prior to widespread vaccine availability, patients having surgery within 7 days of confirmed infection (or who became infected during the subsequent 30 days) had high rates of pulmonary complications (51.2% of patients) and perioperative death (38.0% of patients with pulmonary complications).<sup>203</sup> Data from that time indicated that in patients recovered from COVID- 19, increased risk of mortality<sup>204</sup>

|**Table 29.22**|Organ System Manifestations that Present During Chronic HIV In|fection or After an AIDS Diagnosis|
|---|---|---|
|**Organ System**|**Clinical Manifestation in HIV**|**Clinical Manifestation in AIDS**|
|Cardiovascular|Lipodystrophy, dyslipidemia, coronary artery disease, venous<br>thromboembolic disease, prolonged QT interval, autonomic<br>dysfunction|HFrEF, myocarditis, pericardial effusion, cardiac tumors|
|Neurologic|Neurocognitive dysfunction, depression, peripheral neuropathy|Mania/bipolar disease|
|Hepatic|Co- infection with hepatitis B or C, liver fibrosis or cirrhosis||
|Renal|Chronic kidney disease, medication nephrotoxicity, HIV- associated<br>nephrotoxicity||
|Pulmonary|Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, recurrent pneumonias|Pleural effusions (infectious or malignant), disseminated<br>pulmonary infections (e.g.,_Mycobacterium avium_<br>complex [MAC]), tuberculosis infection|
|Endocrine|Diabetes mellitus, metabolic disorder|Adrenal insufficiency|


_AIDS_ , acquired immunodeficiency syndrome; _HFrEF_ , heart failure with reduced ejection fraction; _HIV_ , human immunodeficiency virus; _MAC_ , _Mycobacterium avium_ complex.



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 42 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 42 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0042.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 43 -->

and postoperative pneumonia<sup>205</sup> persisted for 7–8 weeks after diagnosis. The risk of postoperative pneumonia, respiratory failure, pulmonary embolism, and sepsis was highest in the first 0–4 weeks following COVID- 19 diagnosis.<sup>205</sup> Elective surgery is delayed during the acute phase of illness and non- operative alternatives are pursued in emergency situations with symptomatic COVID- 19. In 2022, several societies recommended delaying elective or scheduled surgery for at least 7 weeks after diagnosis, unless the risks of delaying surgery outweigh the increased potential for morbidity or mortality.<sup>206a</sup> As vaccination rates have increased and the severity of disease has decreased, it is now considered safe to proceed with elective surgery 2 weeks after COVID-19 infection.<sup>206b</sup>

A significant proportion of patients develop persistent symptoms after acute COVID- 19 (termed post- acute COVID19 syndrome or “long COVID”).<sup>207</sup> The understanding of long COVID is still evolving. Known potential sequelae are listed in Table 29.23.<sup>207</sup> In patients who do not experience complete resolution of symptoms, preoperative assessment focuses on stability of disease and planning for optimal timing for surgery.

**Table 29.23** Organ System Manifestations of PostAcute COVID- 19 Syndrome

|**Organ System**|**Manifestations**|
|---|---|
|Pulmonary|ᑏDyspnea, decreased exercise capacity,<br>hypoxia<br>ᑏReduced diffusion capacity, restrictive<br>pulmonary physiology, ground-glass<br>opacities, fibrotic changes<br>ᑏAssessment by home pulse oximeter,<br>6- minute walk test, pulmonary function test,<br>chest computed tomography|
|Hematologic|ᑏThromboembolic events<br>ᑏHyperinflammatory state<br>ᑏAssessment by elevated D- dimer levels|
|Cardiovascular|ᑏPalpitations, dyspnea, chest pain<br>ᑏIncreased cardiometabolic demand,<br>myocardial fibrosis or scarring, arrythmias,<br>tachycardia, and autonomic dysfunction<br>ᑏAssessment by echocardiogram,<br>electrocardiogram, or cardiac magnetic<br>resonance imaging|
|Neuropsychiatric|ᑏFatigue, myalgia, headache, dysautonomia,<br>cognitive impairment (“brain fog”)<br>ᑏAnxiety, depression, sleep disturbance, post-<br>traumatic stress disorder|
|Renal|ᑏAcute kidney injury, prolonged reduced<br>eGFR, COVAN|
|Endocrine|ᑏNew or worsening diabetes mellitus,<br>thyroiditis, bone demineralization<br>ᑏAssessment for hypothalamic–pituitary–<br>adrenal axis suppression or hyperthyroidism|
|Gastrointestinal and<br>hepatobiliary|ᑏProlonged viral fecal shedding<br>ᑏAlteration of gut microbiome|
|Dermatologic|ᑏHair loss|


### Intraoperative

Whether anesthetic type has an effect on outcomes in patients with COVID- 19 is unknown. A large prospective cohort study of patients having surgery for proximal femur fractures with perioperative COVID- 19 demonstrated no difference in mortality between anesthetic type, despite a high overall 30- day mortality of 29.4%.<sup>208</sup> Intraoperative management and medication selection takes into account the sequelae of disease present. Anesthetic type is selected based the most appropriate plan for the surgery and patient health status, regardless of COVID- 19 status.

### Postoperative

While it is recognized that COVID- 19 is associated with a prothrombotic state, there is no consensus for venous thromboembolism (VTE) prophylaxis recommendations following surgery.<sup>209</sup> Risk assessment for perioperative VTE considers COVID- 19 within the previous 30 days as an additional factor.

## Genetic Diseases

Patients with genetic disorders frequently have multisystem dysfunction and dysmorphic physical features with anesthetic implications. Syndromes identified in childhood often necessitate management in specialized clinics or by pediatricians knowledgeable in the disorder. If continuing care is not established, patients can be lost to follow- up after 18 years of age. Features of the syndrome often worsen over time as the patient ages, requiring ongoing reassessment with each anesthetic, whether surgery is for sequelae of their syndrome or for an unrelated problem. In patients with congenital heart disease, the natural history of the surgical repair is factored into preoperative assessment. For example, patients with tetralogy of Fallot (TOF) usually undergo definitive surgery prior to 1 year of age with correction of right ventricular outflow tract (RVOT) obstruction and separation of pulmonary and systemic circulation through closure of the ventricular septal defect (VSD).<sup>210</sup> Depending on the surgical approach to RVOT enlargement, most patients are left with chronic severe pulmonary regurgitation that causes RV enlargement over time. Patients may also have residual RVOT obstruction. Pulmonic valve replacement is often necessary to avoid RV failure from chronic volume overload. The optimal timing for surgical intervention is unclear, and reassessment of RV function prior to elective surgery may be warranted. Over 40% of patients with repaired TOF have arrythmias, with the prevalence increasing after 45 years of age.<sup>211</sup> If the patient’s clinical history is suspicious of underlying arrythmias, the high prevalence of disease in this population makes preoperative testing more fruitful. Further details about the management of anesthesia in pediatric patients is found in Chapter 72. See Chapter 73 for further details about congenital heart disease.

### DOWN SYNDROME

_COVAN_ , coronavirus- associated nephropathy; _COVID- 19_ , coronavirus 2019; _eGFR_ , estimated glomerular filtration rate. Adapted from Nalbandian A, Sehgal K, Gupta A, et al. Post- acute COVID- 19 syndrome. _Nat Med_ . 2021;27(4):601–615.

### Preoperative

Down syndrome is the most common chromosomal abnormality in humans, caused by an extra chromosome



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 43 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 43 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0043.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 44 -->

inherited in position 21 (i.e., trisomy 21). Individuals with Down syndrome fall along a spectrum of disease; some patients are mildly affected and able to live independent lives as adults, while others have severe cognitive impairment and chronic medical sequelae. Preoperative assessment focuses on determining the severity of individual manifestations of disease and, usually, involving a caregiver or substitute decision- maker for discussions of risks and benefits of proceeding with surgery. See Table 29.24 for a summary of manifestations of Down syndrome. There is an increased incidence of atlantoaxial instability (AAI) in patients with Down syndrome; however, screening radiographs for prediction of disease have limited utility. Since 2011, the American Academy of Pediatrics advocates for screening radiographic evaluation only in children with clinical evidence of AAI, such as neck pain, upper extremity weakness, difficulty walking, or change in bowel or bladder function.<sup>212</sup> In patients who have undergone lateral cervical spine radiographs at preschool age, who have normal occiput–C1 alignment, and remain symptom free, no further screening is required.<sup>213</sup> If no prior radiographs have been performed, or a patient has symptoms consistent with AAI, precautions with intubation and neck position mitigates risk. Neurosurgical consultation is advised in patients with evidence of AAI and symptoms consistent with spinal cord dysfunction.

### Intraoperative

Difficult ventilation and intubation are anticipated in patients with Down syndrome due to obesity and orofacial abnormalities (high-arched palate, macroglossia, micrognathia). Congenital lower airway abnormalities are more prevalent in Down syndrome, such as airway malacia, tracheal bronchus, or subglottic stenosis. In a cohort study of Down syndrome patients undergoing endoscopic investigation of chronic respiratory symptoms, 71% of patients had congenital airway abnormalities compared with 32% of controls (patients with

respiratory symptoms but no other medical history).<sup>214</sup> There is also an increased prevalence of dysphonia and vocal fold paralysis than in the general pediatric population, with a link to childhood cardiac surgery and injury of the left recurrent laryngeal nerve hypothesized.<sup>215</sup> Precautions with intubation may include in-line neck stabilization, fiberoptic airway visualization, and a smaller endotracheal tube size than anticipated for age to mitigate risk.

In patients with congenital heart disease, intraoperative management accounts for heart failure, pulmonary hypertension, or intracardiac shunting if present.

### Postoperative

Patients with severe obstructive sleep apnea may need respiratory monitoring after major surgery or if opioid requirement is high. Early-onset dementia puts patients at risk for delirium and cognitive dysfunction after surgery that could limit use of patient- controlled analgesia. Intensive care unit admission may be appropriate.

### OTHER CONGENITAL DISEASES

Several congenital diseases are associated with difficult airway management, along with multiorgan abnormalities.<sup>216</sup> In approaching patients with rare or unfamiliar syndromes, the typical features of the disease are reviewed to determine appropriate preoperative assessment and plan for intraoperative management. Difficulty in airway management may arise from oral cavity or oropharynx abnormalities (e.g., Down syndrome, Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome); mandible or maxilla abnormalities (e.g., Pierre Robin syndrome, Treacher Collins syndrome, Goldenhar syndrome); pharyngeal or tracheal abnormalities (e.g., Down syndrome); cervical spine abnormalities (e.g., Down syndrome, Klippel–Feil syndrome, Goldenhar syndrome); or a combination of factors.<sup>216</sup> See Table 29.25 for clinical features of select genetic diseases with known association of difficult airway management.

**Table 29.24** Summary of Clinical Features Of Down Syndrome and Potential Relevance to Anesthesia

|**Organ System**|**Clinical Feature**|**Perioperative Management**|
|---|---|---|
|**Neuropsychiatric**|ᑏCognitive impairment<br>ᑏAutism<br>ᑏEarly-onset dementia or Alzheimer disease<br>ᑏSeizures|ᑏNeed for substitute decision- maker<br>ᑏLimited ability to cooperate for invasive procedures (e.g.,<br>awake intubation, arterial line placement, light sedation)|
|**Cardiovascular**|ᑏCongenital heart disease: atrioventricular septal defect,<br>ventricular septal defect, atrial septal defect, tetralogy of<br>Fallot, patent ductus arteriosus<br>ᑏPulmonary hypertension|ᑏDeterioration of valvular/septal defect repair leading to heart<br>failure<br>ᑏNeed for infective endocarditis prophylaxis for incomplete or<br>recent repair<br>ᑏHemodynamic instability intraoperatively|
|**Endocrine**|ᑏObesity<br>ᑏHypothyroidism<br>ᑏDiabetes mellitus type 1|ᑏSequelae of obesity<br>ᑏPerioperative glycemic management|
|**Pulmonary**|ᑏObstructive sleep apnea|ᑏPotential difficult airway management, occult pulmonary<br>hypertension|
|**Musculoskeletal**|ᑏAtlantoaxial subluxation/instability<br>ᑏEarly-onset arthropathy<br>ᑏOrofacial abnormalities: high- arched palate, macroglossia,<br>micrognathia|ᑏCaution with intubation and neck instability<br>ᑏPotential difficult intubation<br>ᑏChronic pain|

## Toxic Ingestions and Drug Abuse

### INTRODUCTION

Patients with an acute drug overdose or experiencing effects of a substance ingestion do not routinely undergo anesthesia for surgery. However, critical situations arise that require time- sensitive intervention despite an acute

toxicity. Knowledge of the metabolic effects and anesthetic implications for ingestions is important in the setting of emergency surgery.

### ALCOHOL USE DISORDER

### Preoperative

Patients with alcohol use disorder (AUD) are at increased risk for perioperative mortality, longer length of hospital stay, surgical site infections, postoperative bleeding, and need for secondary surgeries.<sup>217–220</sup> Long- term AUD causes an altered immune response in both cellular and humoral immunity, which is likely one causative factor in adverse events.<sup>220</sup> Alcoholics who consume 80 g of undiluted alcohol per day (approximately equivalent to 6–7 standard drinks) for over 10 years have a reported rate of liver disease of nearly 100%.<sup>221</sup> While often reversible in early stages if abstinence is achieved, up to 20% of patients with steatosis will progress to fibrosis within 10 years if alcohol abuse continues.<sup>221</sup> Patients with cirrhosis are at increased risk of perioperative morbidity and mortality; see the earlier section on Liver Cirrhosis for more details.

In one randomized controlled study of alcoholic patients undergoing elective colorectal surgery, abstinence for 1 month prior to surgery reduced the incidence of myocardial ischemia, arrythmias, and nightly hypoxemic episodes.<sup>222</sup> Improvements in organ dysfunction with short- term abstinence from alcohol is anticipated, however, longterm impact of perioperative interventions on abstinence rates are limited.<sup>223</sup> See Table 29.26 for anticipated time required for recovery of organ function after abstinence. Though preoperative abstinence is the goal, it may not be achievable without supervised addiction intervention. Lorazepam can be prescribed to be taken preoperatively to aid in withdrawal symptom prevention, in order to achieve abstinence. See Chapter 28 for the preoperative workup of patients with history of substance abuse.

### Intraoperative

Patients with AUD have increased anesthetic requirements. The dose of propofol required to induce anesthesia and dose of fentanyl required to achieve analgesic effect is increased in AUD over patients without AUD.<sup>224,225</sup> In

**Table 29.26** Time Required After Abstinence from Alcohol for Recovery of Organ Function

|**Organ System**|**Abstinence Time**|
|---|---|
|Immune competence|2–8 weeks|
|Wound healing|&lt;2 months|
|Endocrine stress response|2–12 weeks|
|Bone regeneration|&lt;6 months|
|Hemostasis|1–4 weeks|
|Cardiac function – without<br>symptoms|1 month|
|Cardiac function – with<br>symptoms|1–6 months|


Adapted from Tonnesen H, Nielsen PR, Lauritzen JB, Moller AM. Smoking and alcohol intervention before surgery: evidence for best practice. _Br J Anaesth_ . 2009;102(3):297–306.



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 45 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 45 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0045.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 46 -->

a research study setting, preoperative infusion of alcohol reduced anesthetic requirements in patients with and without AUD, while also attenuating catecholamine- mediated effects of surgery.<sup>225</sup> Anesthetic depth is titrated according to the degree of current intoxication or length of abstinence time. Mild alcohol withdrawal symptoms begin as early as 6 hours after last alcohol consumption and may manifest perioperatively with tachycardia, hypertension, or fever.

### Postoperative

Alcohol withdrawal is characterized by psychomotor agitation. The Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol (CIWA- A) was revised in 1989 (CIWA- Ar) to include 10 questions for assessment of severity of alcohol withdrawal.<sup>226</sup> The CIWA- Ar is still widely used today and assesses sweating, anxiety, tremor, auditory disturbances, visual disturbances, agitation, nausea, tactile disturbances, headache, and clouding of sensorium. In the postoperative setting, false positives may be common due to overlap with common postanesthetic symptoms.<sup>227</sup> Incorporating the preoperative assessment of alcohol use allows appropriate CIWA- Ar scoring.

For prevention of symptoms of alcohol withdrawal, benzodiazepines remain the mainstay of therapy. Benzodiazepines are primarily effective for preventing withdrawal seizures, though also have beneficial effects on autonomic hyperactivity.<sup>228</sup> Though there is interest in alternative therapies, such as anticonvulsants or baclofen, evidence for efficacy in reducing alcohol withdrawal symptoms is lacking.<sup>229</sup> The addition of dexmedetomidine infusion to benzodiazepines for alcohol withdrawal treatment was associated with increased ICU length of stay, though this may reflect an increased severity of withdrawal symptoms prior to admission.<sup>230</sup> Phenobarbital may be used as an adjunct<sup>231</sup> or alternative<sup>232</sup> to benzodiazepines, though research into efficacy and safety is ongoing. Since benzodiazepines are not indicated for alternative forms of delirium, accurately assessing patients for delirium related to alcohol withdrawal is crucial for appropriate therapy.

### STIMULANT DRUG ABUSE: COCAINE AND METHAMPHETAMINE

### Preoperative

Cocaine and methamphetamine are two examples of stimulant drugs of abuse that act centrally to increase neurotransmitter concentration (dopamine, serotonin, and norepinephrine). Cocaine inhibits presynaptic uptake of neurotransmitters, as well as causing vasoconstriction and sodium channel blockade when applied locally. Methamphetamine acts centrally to increase neurotransmitter release and also inhibits monoamine transporters.<sup>233</sup> Acutely, the sympathomimetic effect causes tachycardia, arrythmias, myocardial vasoconstriction, and hypertension and can lead to acute myocardial ischemia, stroke, or cerebral edema. When taken chronically, both drugs have detrimental effects on the cardiovascular system, causing left ventricular hypertrophy, early atherosclerosis, and heart failure. Methamphetamine also causes pulmonary arterial hypertension by mechanisms that are incompletely understood.<sup>234</sup> Recreational substance use is independently associated with the development of premature atherosclerotic

cardiovascular disease. Risk is increased with cocaine (OR 2.44, 95% CI 2.38–2.50) and amphetamine (OR 2.74, 95% CI 2.62–2.87) as well as the use of tobacco (OR 1.97, 95% CI 1.94–2.00), alcohol (OR 1.50, 95% CI 1.47–1.52), cannabis (OR 2.65, 95% CI 2.59–2.71), and other drugs (OR 2.53, 95% CI 2.47–2.59).<sup>235</sup> Polysubstance use is associated with the greatest risk, with a 9- fold increased risk.<sup>235</sup> See the earlier section on Cardiovascular Diseases for the perioperative management of pulmonary hypertension. See Chapter 28 for the preoperative workup of patients with a history of substance abuse. Transthoracic echocardiography is used for preoperative assessment in patients with suspicion of cardiac complications from stimulant drug use.

### Intraoperative

Hemodynamic management for the chronic cocaine or methamphetamine user is tailored to the presence of cardiovascular disease, if present. See the earlier sections on heart failure, ischemic heart disease, pulmonary hypertension, and stroke for more details.

Acute stimulant intoxication causes hypertension, tachyarrhythmias, and myocardial ischemia with coronary vasoconstriction. A systematic review of pharmacologic treatment of cocaine toxicity provides recommendations based on the limited evidence available (Table 29.27).<sup>236</sup> While the review was tailored to literature surrounding cocaine use, methamphetamine intoxication causes similar cardiovascular effects, so similar principles likely apply.<sup>237</sup>

**Table 29.27** Recommendations for Management of Acute Cocaine Toxicity from a Systematic Review of the Pharmacologic Treatment of Cocaine Toxicity<sup>236</sup>

|**Medication**|**Recommendation**|
|---|---|
|**Benzodiazepines and other**<br>**GABA- active medications**|Helpful as adjuncts for agitation, but<br>do not fully mitigate tachycardia,<br>hypertension, or vasospasm|
|**Calcium channel blockers**|Decrease hypertension and<br>reverse coronary vasospasm, not<br>necessarily tachycardia|
|**Nitric oxide–mediated**<br>**vasodilators**|Nitroglycerin may lead to severe<br>hypotension and reflex<br>tachycardia|
|**Alpha- adrenoceptor blocking**<br>**drugs**|Alpha- 1 blockers may improve<br>hypertension and vasospasm, not<br>tachycardia|
|**Alpha- 2- adrenoceptor**<br>**agonists**|Evidence for efficacy of<br>dexmedetomidine|
|**Beta blockers and beta/alpha**<br>**blockers**|Adverse events associated with<br>selective and nonselective beta-<br>blocker administration<br>No adverse events reported for<br>combined beta/alpha blockers<br>(labetalol, carvedilol), effective for<br>hypertension and tachycardia|
|**Antipsychotics**|Improve agitation and psychosis,<br>inconsistent effect on tachycardia<br>and hypertension, risk of<br>extrapyramidal effects|
|**Other agents**|Morphine reverses coronary<br>vasoconstriction|


_GABA_ , gamma-aminobutyric acid.



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 46 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 46 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0046.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 47 -->

Caution with beta- blocker administration is advised to avoid precipitating extreme hypertension from unopposed α- adrenergic stimulation.<sup>236</sup> Sympathomimetics, such as ketamine, may exacerbate symptoms and are avoided when possible. Caution with nasogastric tubes, nasopharyngeal airways, or nasal temperature probes is advised, as septal perforation may be present from chronic nasal cocaine use. Chronic methamphetamine use causes severe dental decay and periodontitis<sup>238</sup> ; caution with intubation is required to avoid further dental trauma or dislodging teeth.

In the absence of acute intoxication, chronic use leads to catecholamine depletion and intraoperative hypotension is common.

### Postoperative

Patients with stimulant withdrawal may be restless, anxious, or somnolent, making opioid titration challenging. If cardiovascular disease is present, postoperative hemodynamic management is tailored to parameters appropriate to the condition.

### DRUG OVERDOSE

Patients with drug overdoses often present with nonspecific symptoms, such as nausea, vomiting, and decreased level of consciousness. If the clinical scenario suggests a possible intentional or unintentional overdose, identifying characteristic features of an overdose may aid in providing rapid therapy. Diagnosis and treatment of a drug overdose are coordinated with a medical toxicologist or poison control

center. Basic knowledge of the general principles of treatment can aid in prompt management if a drug overdose is encountered perioperatively in an emergency surgical setting. If untreated, many drug overdoses lead to severe toxicity with hemodynamic instability, end-organ dysfunction, and cardiac arrest. See Table 29.28 for an overview of drug overdose in select medications.

## Allergy and Anaphylaxis

The lifetime prevalence of anaphylaxis is 1.6%–5.1%.<sup>239</sup> Risk factors for severe anaphylaxis include cardiovascular disease, asthma, older age, and coexisting comorbidities. The grading of severity of hypersensitivity reactions is shown in Table 29.29.<sup>240</sup> The common culprits in perioperative hypersensitivity events vary among countries and over time.<sup>241</sup> Antibiotics are the most commonly reported cause in most countries. Blue dyes used for sentinel node procedures and chlorhexidine are increasing in frequency.<sup>241</sup> Latex, previously a common cause, is now decreasing in prevalence due to decreased exposure. Neuromuscular blocking agents (NMBA) were thought to be the most common cause in all countries, but the high incidence of NMBA anaphylaxis is more common in countries where pholcodine- containing cough remedies are available (e.g., France, Australia, United Kingdom, and Belgium). This is due to a structural similarity between pholcodine and NMBA.<sup>241</sup> There were no cases of proven local anesthetic allergic cases reported in the 6th

|**Medication**|**Clinical Features of Overdose**|**Treatment Principles**|
|---|---|---|
|**Acetaminophen**|ᑏEarly (0.5 to 24 hours): nonspecific symptoms such as<br>nausea, vomiting, lethargy<br>ᑏLater (24–72 hours): hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity,<br>elevation in aminotransferases|ᑏDetermine time since ingestion, measure serum<br>acetaminophen level, administer_N_- acetylcysteine<br>according to Rumack–Matthew nomogram|

|**Tricyclic antidepressant**|ᑏSedation, delirium, hallucinations, seizure<br>ᑏTachycardia, hypotension, conduction abnormalities<br>ᑏAnticholinergic toxicity: dilated pupils, dry mouth,<br>urinary retention, hyperthermia<br>ᑏECG abnormalities: QRS duration >100 ms deep S wave<br>in leads I, AVL, tall R wave in lead AVR|ᑏIntubation for airway protection<br>ᑏSodium bicarbonate for cardiac toxicity with widened QRS<br>ᑏSupportive management for hypotension and seizures<br>(avoid sodium channel blockers, like phenytoin)<br>ᑏConsider lipid emulsion for refractory cardiovascular<br>instability, caution with all antiarrhythmics other than<br>lidocaine and magnesium|

|---|---|---|
|1 or 2|ᑏGeneralized urticaria<br>ᑏExtensive erythema<br>ᑏ± Angioedema<br>ᑏDiarrhea, vomiting|ᑏNo epinephrine needed<br>ᑏAntihistamines|
|3<br>Mild–moderate anaphylaxis|ᑏMild–moderate hypotension<br>ᑏTachycardia or bradycardia<br>ᑏModerate bronchospasm<br>ᑏNeurologic symptoms|ᑏEpinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg in adults and 0.3 mg in<br>children) (thigh > deltoid)<br>ᑏ0.5 L crystalloid bolus, repeat if no response|
|4<br>Severe anaphylaxis|ᑏSevere hypotension<br>ᑏTachycardia or bradycardia<br>ᑏSevere angioedema<br>ᑏSevere bronchospasm, stridor<br>ᑏSevere GI symptoms<br>ᑏSevere mucocutaneous signs<br>ᑏGCS 13–14|ᑏEpinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg in adults and 0.3 mg in<br>children); if no response, then epinephrine infusion<br>ᑏ1 L crystalloid bolus, repeat if no response<br>ᑏVasopressin if persistent hypotension more than 10 min<br>ᑏChest compressions for MAP &lt;50 mm Hg or ETCO2&lt;20 mm Hg<br>ᑏGlucagon if on beta blockers|
|5|ᑏCardiopulmonary arrest<br>ᑏGCS &lt;13|ᑏAdvanced cardiac life support methods|


_ETCO2_ , end- tidal carbon dioxide; _GCS_ , Glasgow Coma Score; _GI_ , gastrointestinal; _IM_ , intramuscular; _L_ , liters; _MAP_ , mean arterial pressure.

National Audit Project of the Royal College of Anaes thetists (NAP6) survey or two other recent studies of perioperative anaphylaxis.<sup>241</sup> Ester local anesthetics (e.g., procaine, tetracaine) are more antigenic than amides (e.g., lidocaine, bupivacaine, ropivacaine). The para- aminobenzoic acid metabolite of esters is responsible for much of the antigenicity.

### Preoperative

A careful history to determine true allergies from adverse outcomes or expected, known side effects (e.g., nausea and vomiting with opioids or general anesthesia) is incredibly helpful to plan care. Chronic spontaneous urticaria affects 1% of the population at any given time. It is characterized by recurrent episodes of urticaria or angioedema, usually provoked by stress, and often exacerbated during surgery. Allergy investigations should ideally be performed by specialized allergists. Testing includes all drugs given intravenously less than 1 hour before the reaction, and all substances given orally, intramuscularly, subcutaneously, epidurally, spinally, and topically less than 2 hours before the reaction.<sup>240</sup> Exposure to latex and disinfectants is presumed in all patients and routinely tested. Chlorhexidine is responsible for up to 10% of cases.<sup>242</sup> If the culprit is an antibiotic, NMBA, or local anesthetic, testing for cross- reactivity should be performed to identify safe alternatives.<sup>240</sup> Often if records are lacking or there is not a clear culprit, an approach to safe care is to test a subset of anesthetic agents to identify a list of "safe" medications with low likelihood to cause an allergic reaction.

Antihistamine and glucocorticoid premedication are effective in specific chemotherapy protocols, but evidence is lacking that antihistamines and glucocorticoid premedication in patients receiving low- or iso- osmolar contrast material and other situations prevent recurrent anaphylaxis.<sup>239</sup> However, in contrast to immediate reactions, premedication may prevent severe, delayed, cutaneous T- cell- mediated reactions to radiographic contrast.

### Intraoperative

Propofol is safe to use in patients with peanut, soy, or egg allergy.<sup>243</sup> True hypersensitivity reactions to local anesthetics are considered rare. Cefazolin allergy is generally

selective, and rarely associated with cross- reactivity to penicillins or other cephalosporins.<sup>244</sup> All NMBA have been reported to be causal agents. Sugammadex carries a risk of 0.002% anaphylaxis per administration. NSAIDs are rare but known causes of perioperative hypersensitivity. Chlorhexidine topically, as a lubricant for urinary catheters, and chlorhexidine- impregnated central venous catheters have been implicated in reactions.<sup>242</sup> Anaphylaxis to dyes is often delayed in onset compared with intravenous antigens. Hypersensitivity reactions to colloids have decreased because of the withdrawal of, or restrictions of, gelatins, dextrans, and hydroxyethyl starch. Hypersensitivity to blood products occurs in 0.6 per 1000 transfusions.<sup>245</sup> Platelets cause 1.1 allergic reactions per 1000 transfusions, plasma 0.68/1000 transfusions, and 0.04/1000 for red cell concentrates.

There is marked variability in clinical presentation of hypersensitivity reactions in patients receiving anesthesia. Severe hypersensitivity reactions are challenging to diagnose because hemodynamic instability is often attributed to anesthetics or surgical side effects. Certain drugs mimic hypersensitivity reactions if administered rapidly or in large doses (e.g., oxytocin and vancomycin). More common causes are airway trauma, angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors, or hereditary angioedema. Major cardiovascular findings were the sole presenting feature in 50% of cases in NAP6, with isolated respiratory symptoms in 18% of cases.<sup>241</sup> Skin signs, considered the hallmark of hypersensitivity, are often absent. Flushing, urticaria, and angioedema may become apparent once intravascular volume and perfusion are restored or the surgical drapes removed.

Epinephrine is the first- line pharmacotherapy for anaphylaxis.<sup>239</sup> Epinephrine, 0.01 mg/kg of a 1:1000 (1 mg/mL) solution to a maximum of 0.5 mg in adults and 0.3 mg in children intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh or the deltoid if the thigh is not accessible is the first- line treatment for anaphylaxis.<sup>239</sup> Intravenous epinephrine is not recommended as first- line treatment of acute anaphylaxis, even in a medical setting, due to risk for adverse cardiac events such as arrhythmias and ischemia.<sup>246</sup> If there is inadequate response to intramuscular epinephrine and fluids, intravenous epinephrine can be given by continuous infusion with



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 48 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 48 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0048.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 49 -->

a pump. Experts advocate intramuscular rather than subcutaneous routes, though for years subcutaneous delivery was the mainstay, without evidence that it was ineffective. Unlike epinephrine, antihistamines do not effectively treat cardiovascular and respiratory symptoms such as hypotension or bronchospasm. Although glucocorticoids are frequently used as adjunctive therapy, evidence is lacking for their benefit. Steroids should not be administered in place of epinephrine in episodes of acute anaphylaxis.

Fluid administration is a key therapy. Consensus recommends a bolus of 0.5 L of crystalloid solution for grade 2 and 1 L for grade 3 reactions, repeated as clinically indicated. Fluid responsiveness should be assessed through stroke volume and cardiac output monitoring in severe cases.<sup>247</sup> Cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) should begin when end- tidal carbon dioxide (ETCO2) falls below 20 mm Hg because this is a marker of inadequate perfusion. Advanced cardiac life support (ACLS) guidelines are followed as indicated. Sugammadex has been reported to be adjunct treatment for rocuronium- induced anaphylaxis.<sup>248</sup> Glucocorticoids have a limited role in acute management because of their slow onset of action, and inability to reverse acute symptoms. Antihistamines are effective for patients with urticaria and can treat many of the cutaneous signs and symptoms of anaphylaxis (e.g., pruritus, flushing, urticaria), but unlike epinephrine, antihistamines are not effective for hypotension or bronchospasm.

The decision to continue with surgery weighs the urgency of surgery, the severity of symptoms, and the patient’s medical history (e.g., underlying cardiovascular disease or use of beta blockers). Distinguishing biphasic anaphylaxis (with an interval of resolution) from protracted anaphylaxis is important. Biphasic anaphylaxis is recurrent episodes 1–72 hours after resolution of the initial episode and occurs in up to 20% of patients. Antihistamines and glucocorticoids do not reliably prevent biphasic anaphylaxis.

A serum tryptase which is released from degranulated mast cells should be measured during a suspected hypersensitivity event to help with subsequent allergy investigation. Accurate documentation of the events, including symptoms, treatments, and the chronology of drug administrations including non- intravenous exposures, such as disinfectants, dyes, gels, sprays, cements, hemostatic agents, bone substitutes, is critically important to assist with future diagnosis and care planning.

### Postoperative

Severe anaphylaxis or the need for repeated doses of epinephrine are risk factors for biphasic anaphylaxis. After diagnosis and treatment of anaphylaxis, all patients should be kept under observation until symptoms have fully resolved. Patients should receive education on anaphylaxis and risk of recurrence and how to avoid triggers. They need to be provided with self- injectable epinephrine and referred to an allergist.

## Hematologic Disorders

### ANEMIA

Anemia is defined as a hemoglobin (Hb) concentration less than 12 g/dL for women and less than 13 g/dL for men.

Anemia can be classified based on multiple criteria (e.g., mean corpuscular volume [MCV]). Most common causes of anemia preoperatively include iron deficiency anemia, anemia of inflammation, and chronic blood loss.

The most important perioperative adverse effects of anemia are the reduction in arterial oxygen concentration and the potential for decreased tissue oxygen delivery. Generally, a compensatory increase in cardiac output (through increased sympathetic stimulation) offsets the decrease in oxygen content. Anemia triggers a rightward shift of the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve, facilitating the release of oxygen from Hb. Another compensatory mechanism is represented by the redistribution of blood flow to essential organs (heart, brain) with a reflex decrease in blood flow to muscles, skin, and kidneys. Patients with coronary artery disease cannot usually compensate for anemia with a further increase in cardiac output resulting in fatigue and poor exercise tolerance. Patients with severe chronic anemia can develop signs of high- output heart failure. Anemia is an independent risk for adverse perioperative outcomes, and is a “disease modifier,” increasing risks associated with the elderly and other comorbidities such as cancer, heart failure, ischemic heart disease, COPD, CKD, and more.

### Preoperative

Patients with anemia should have the cause determined preoperatively. Ferritin, iron concentration, transferrin concentration, vitamin B12, TSH, and creatinine are initial screening tests to evaluate anemia.<sup>249</sup> Patients detected to have iron deficiency or anemia of inflammation should be treated preoperatively (iron infusions for iron deficiency anemia, erythropoietin for anemia associated with chronic kidney disease; see Chapter 28). Patients with chronic anemia should generally avoid preoperative transfusions, even if it is anticipated that patients will require transfusions intraoperatively. Surgical blood loss at a lower Hb concentration will decrease the number of transfusions required perioperatively. Patients with hemoglobinopathies such as sickle cell disease require evaluation and possibly preoperative transfusions in collaboration with a hematologist.

### Intraoperative

Intraoperative anemia management is aimed at preventing tissue hypoxia due to a decrease in oxygen delivery. Blood transfusions portend certain risks. Red blood cell (RBC) transfusions are associated with direct transmission of infectious pathogens such as hepatitis B, hepatitis C, and human immunodeficiency virus (HIV). In critically ill and trauma patients, transfusions are independently associated with increased intensive care unit and hospital lengths of stay, an increased incidence of ventilator- associated pneumonia, and increased mortality. In addition to transfusion reactions, patients can experience allergic side effects, and adverse cardiac or immunomodulatory effects. The immunomodulatory effects of RBC transfusions can lead to cancer recurrence, postoperative bacterial infections, transfusion- related acute lung injury, and hemolytic transfusion reactions. Blood transfusions remain an option for certain patients. The decision to transfuse perioperatively is complex and relies on the amount of blood loss, current or predicted hemoglobin concentration, ongoing bleeding,

<!-- PDF page 50 -->

and evidence of inadequate oxygenation (e.g., cardiac ischemia or renal insufficiency).

The Hb concentration warranting transfusion is a matter of debate, but it is well accepted that there is no benefit of transfusion with a Hb concentration above 10 g/dL whereas even young healthy patients derive benefit when their Hb drops below 6 g/dL. In the perioperative period the decision to transfuse patients with intermediate concentrations of Hb between 6 and 10 g/dL is individualized. The best supportive evidence for a restrictive transfusion strategy comes from the Transfusion Requirements in Critical Care (TRICC) trial, which found no significant difference in 30- day mortality rates between a restrictive transfusion strategy (Hb 7–8 g/ dL) and a liberal strategy (Hb maintained at 10–12 g/dL).<sup>250</sup> The restrictive regime was not associated with a significant increase in mortality, cardiac morbidity, or duration of hospitalization. Other studies also show no short- or long- term mortality benefit when a liberal transfusion strategy (transfusion when Hb less than 10 g/dL) versus a more restrictive strategy (i.e., transfusion when Hb less than 8 g/dL) is used in patients with underlying coronary artery disease undergoing hip surgery. In patients with hip fractures, a restrictive transfusion threshold is associated with a higher risk of acute coronary syndromes while a liberal strategy increased the risk of cerebrovascular accidents.<sup>251</sup> However, a restrictive transfusion plan may not benefit patients undergoing cardiac surgery. Recent data demonstrated a higher mortality rate, but no increase in serious postoperative ischemic events in cardiac surgery patients with a restrictive transfusion strategy (Hb less than 7.5 g/dL) versus a liberal one (transfusion when Hb less than 10 g/dL).<sup>252,253</sup> In patients with active coronary syndromes (unstable angina or acute myocardial infarction), the literature suggests that a hematocrit of 28%–30% as an appropriate transfusion trigger.

### Postoperative

The same intraoperative principles apply to management of anemic patients postoperatively. In addition, patients receiving massive transfusions are at additional risks such as transfusion- associated lung injury (TRALI) or transfusion- associated circulatory overload (TACO).

### SICKLE CELL ANEMIA

The sickle cell syndromes constitute a family of hemoglobinopathies caused by abnormal β- globin chain (valine is exchanged for glutamic acid). Sickle cell disease is an autosomal recessive disorder. The sickling results from aggregation of deoxygenated Hb S distorting the cell membrane and producing the abnormal shape. Sickle cells aggregate in blood vessels, and cause tissue infarcts by impeding blood flow and oxygen to tissues. Several variants of sickle cell disease exist: homozygous sickle cell disease (Hb SS), sickle cell–thalassemia disease (Hb SC) with clinical features similar to Hb SS, or sickle cell trait (Hb AS). Patients with sickle cell trait do not have clinically significant sickling because Hb AS cells aggregate when the oxygen saturation of Hb is less than 20%. Survival of patients with sickle cell trait is similar to persons with normal Hb, but patients with Hb AS have a 50% increase in pulmonary infarction. For patients with Hb SS (or SC), the pathologic endorgan damage is due to three processes. Vaso- occlusive

crises due to sickling in blood vessels cause infarcts and ischemia- induced tissue destruction. These crises generally present as bone and joint pain during stressful periods (such as illnesses), but infarcts can lead to damage to the spleen, kidneys (hematuria with inability to concentrate urine and progression to renal failure), and brain (cerebral infarcts in adolescents, hemorrhagic in adults). Acute chest syndrome is a cause of morbidity and mortality in patients with sickle cell disease who present with an acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS)- type clinical course, with bilateral infiltrates, chest pain, fever, and wheezing. Hemolytic crises result in an acute drop in Hb levels accompanied by decreased haptoglobin concentrations, increased lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) and indirect bilirubin, and a high reticulocyte count. Aplastic crises present with a decrease in Hb concentrations in the setting of a low reticulocyte count.

### Preoperative

Sickle cell disease is the cause of multiorgan dysfunction including neurocognitive dysfunction, stroke, hypertension, acute and chronic kidney disease, and cardiac and pulmonary complications. Patients with sickle cell disease often have chronic pain and depression. Patients are highly susceptible to bacterial and viral infections, due to functional asplenia that occurs in childhood. Individuals often have vitamin and micronutrient deficiencies (see earlier section on Malnutrition). Pulmonary hypertension is a frequent and severe complication, and independently predicts mortality. It can be present already in children and occurs in patients with Hb SC disease as well as Hb SS anemia. Common symptoms of pulmonary hypertension such as dyspnea on exertion, fatigue, chest pain, peripheral edema, syncope, and palpitations are frequent in patients with sickle cell disease with and without pulmonary hypertension. Echocardiographic screening every 1–3 years is indicated before all but minor procedures. NT-proBNP is elevated in patients with pulmonary hypertension, and correlates with severity. NT-proBNP can be used in addition to echocardiography. A 6MWT of less than 333 meters, and an NT-proBNP greater than 159 pg/mL may improve the echocardiographic accuracy of edema, syncope, and palpitations, which are frequent in patients with sickle cell disease with and without pulmonary hypertension. Patients should have magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) for liver iron content every 1 to 2 years rather than assessment through ferritin concentrations alone in individuals who receive repeated transfusions.

Patients with sickle cell trait do well perioperatively similarly to patients with Hb A, therefore no additional interventions are indicated. Patients with sickle cell disease should not have surgeries during crises, except for emergencies. Patients should continue routine medications used to treat their anemia. Hydroxyurea stimulates the production of hemoglobin F and releases nitric oxide. This prevents vaso- occlusive crises and acute chest syndrome. Lower leg painful ulcers are a common adverse cutaneous effect and reported to occur in 10% of patients undergoing long- term hydroxyurea treatment. Hydroxyurea should be stopped in patients with ulcers. Crizanlizumab is a monoclonal antibody against P- selectin, an adhesion molecule on platelets and endothelial cells. L- glutamine alters the redox state of RBCs. These are options to decrease vaso- occlusive crises in

<!-- PDF page 51 -->

patients not tolerating or on optimal doses of hydroxyurea. Voxelotor, an agent that reduces Hb polymerization, may reduce hemolysis.<sup>254</sup>

Preoperative planning depends on the surgical stress of the procedure. High stress levels can precipitate crises. Minor procedures less than 30–60 minutes are low risk. Most intra- abdominal (urologic, gynecologic), head and neck, and orthopedic surgery are considered intermediate risk. Cardiac, major vascular, intrathoracic, neurosurgery, and transplant surgeries are considered high risk for developing vaso- occlusive crises. Generally, patients having lowrisk procedures can proceed without further optimization. Measures should be taken to decrease the risk of sickling for patients undergoing intermediate and high- risk procedures. There is debate as to best practices in patients at high risk for sickling. The Transfusion Alternatives Preoperatively in Sickle Cell Disease (TAPS) study compared no preoperative transfusion with transfusion in patients having low- and moderate- risk surgeries. Transfusion was associated with lower risk of acute chest syndrome and other important complications postoperatively, but no difference in vaso- occlusive crises.<sup>255</sup> Another multicenter trial comparing preoperative transfusion to Hb 10 g/dL to the traditional approach with exchange transfusion to decrease the percentage of Hb SS to less than 30% found no benefit to the exchange approach.<sup>256</sup> The decision to transfuse considers the baseline total Hb, complications with prior transfusions, disease severity, and the stress of surgery. Transfusions are administered to achieve a Hb of more than 9 g/dL before surgery. Red cell exchange transfusions are indicated for patients who require preoperative transfusion but have an Hb greater than 9–10 g/dL that precludes simple transfusion.<sup>257</sup> Many of these patients have red cell alloimmunization from frequent blood transfusions. It is advisable to do a type and screen even for procedures with a low risk of transfusion to ensure availability of blood products in case of unexpected blood loss or to treat a hemolytic crisis.

### Intraoperative

All anesthetic techniques are acceptable for patients with sickle cell disease. Perioperative anxiety, dehydration, hypothermia, hypoxia, and acidosis are avoided to reduce sickling. Tourniquets should be avoided if possible to avoid metabolic acidosis and regional hypoxemia.<sup>258</sup> Neuraxial and regional anesthetics offer the advantage of superior pain management, especially in a patient population who is generally opioid tolerant. The resulting vasodilation may decrease sickling. When contrast is needed for imaging procedures, hypo- or iso- osmolar agents should be used to prevent sickling possible with hyperosmolar solutions.

### Postoperative

Optimal pain control, and prevention of dehydration, hypothermia, hypoxia, acidosis, and infections are the basis of postoperative care. Monitoring of Hb and renal function are important. Incentive spirometry is used to minimize complications. Venous thromboprophylaxis is indicated.<sup>258</sup> Acute chest syndrome may develop 2–3 days postoperatively, and requires supportive treatment of hypoxemia, pain, infection, and hypovolemia. Volume overload should be avoided. While mild cases may respond to simple transfusion, exchange transfusion may be needed in severely affected patients.

### THALASSEMIA

In thalassemia, globin structures are normal, but the synthesis of either the α or β chains of hemoglobin (α- and β- thalassemia, respectively) decreases. Patients with thalassemia minor (α or β trait) have a mild microcytic anemia with few implications for perioperative care. Those with thalassemia intermedia have more pronounced microcytosis and degrees of anemia. They have sequelae from multiple transfusions including iron overload, hepatosplenomegaly, cardiomegaly, pulmonary hypertension, and hypercoagulability (β- thalassemia intermedia).<sup>259</sup>

Patients with thalassemia major require repeated transfusions to correct anemia and suppress ineffective erythropoiesis. These patients have extramedullary hematopoiesis (frontal bossing, maxillary overgrowth, growth retardation), hepatosplenomegaly, heart failure (due to biventricular dilation and pulmonary hypertension), and iron overload (cirrhosis, right- sided heart failure, endocrinopathy).<sup>260,261</sup> These patients are generally transfusion- dependent, and benefit from monitoring of iron stores and chelation therapy (desferrioxamine). Luspatercept reduces transfusion requirements in adults.<sup>262</sup> Splenectomy is reserved for patients with hypersplenism or increasing transfusion demand, since it results in an increased infections and thromboembolic risk.

### Preoperative

Anemia should be corrected to an Hb of 10–11 g/dL preoperatively.<sup>263</sup> In addition, sequelae involving different organs such as the heart (cardiomegaly, heart failure), lungs (pulmonary hypertension), liver (due to iron overload), kidney, and endocrine (diabetes, thyroid function) should be identified and optimized preoperatively as much as possible.

### Intraoperative

Difficulty with intubation is anticipated when maxillary hypertrophy is present and SGA placement may be problematic in patients with high-arched palates. All anesthetic techniques are acceptable and have been safely used. Clinicians should be aware of skeletal abnormalities which may pose technical difficulties with neuraxial and regional techniques. In patients with high- output heart failure, volatile anesthetics and other cardiodepressant agents may require adjustments. Monitoring is dictated by the invasiveness of the procedure, and comorbidities. Caution is exercised during patient positioning, because of associated osteoporosis and leg ulcers. Patients having splenectomy are at risk of intraoperative hypertension due to surgical manipulation of the spleen and autotransfusion.<sup>264</sup>

### Postoperative

Appropriate thromboprophylaxis is provided as thalassemia patients are prone to clotting. Splenectomy is an independent risk for VTE.<sup>265</sup>

### GLUCOSE- 6- PHOSPHATE DEHYDROGENASE DEFICIENCY

Glucose- 6- phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency is an X- linked enzymatic disorder of red blood cells, with premature hemolysis (half- life of G6PD- deficient red cells is approximately 60 days). Depending on the amount of enzyme, G6PD
