deficiency presents as a spectrum from chronic hemolytic anemia (present with less than 10% G6PD activity), intermittent stress- induced hemolysis (10%–60% G6PD activity) to no hemolysis (greater than 60% G6PH activity).<sup>266</sup> G6PD- deficient red cells are susceptible to oxidative damage secondary to certain drug ingestion, infections, or fava bean ingestion, resulting in hemolysis (Table 29.30).

### Preoperative

Generally, no special evaluations are indicated in patients with G6PD deficiency. However, knowing the amount of enzyme activity as well as a prior history of hemolytic crisis is helpful in planning the anesthetic.

### Intraoperative

The mainstay of management depends on the severity and acuity of the anemia and relies on avoiding medications that can trigger hemolysis.<sup>266</sup> Benzodiazepines are safe. Propofol, fentanyl, and ketamine may inhibit G6PD activity _in vitro_ , but not _in vivo_ , and therefore are considered safe to use. It is unclear whether inhalational anesthetics are safe. Drugs that can induce methemoglobinemia are avoided (benzocaine, lidocaine, and prilocaine). Hypothermia, acidosis, and hyperglycemia can trigger hemolysis and should be avoided.<sup>267</sup> Intraoperative hemolysis is difficult to detect because the immediate signs including hypotension can be attributed to other causes. Red- colored urine from free Hb is an indication of a hemolytic reaction. The presumed offending agent should be discontinued, and urinary output maintained with crystalloid solution and diuretics.<sup>267</sup>

### Postoperative

Hemolytic crises can occur 1–3 days after a triggering event. Patients should be monitored for cyanosis, tachycardia, dyspnea, abdominal pain, dark urine discoloration, and jaundice.<sup>267</sup>

### POLYCYTHEMIA

Polycythemia is an increase in RBC mass and hematocrit above normal. Polycythemia is either primary (polycythemia vera) due to a clonal proliferation of RBC precursors from the bone marrow in the setting of a _JAK2_ gene mutation, or secondary (from hypoxia or erythropoietin-producing tumors). The optimal hematocrit is 33%–36%. The pathophysiology of polycythemia is due to the increase in blood viscosity, which rises exponentially when the hematocrit reaches 55%–60%. The cerebral circulation is most significantly impaired by the hyperviscosity.<sup>266</sup> When the hematocrit is above 60%, patients complain of fatigue and headaches, and are at significant risk of venous and arterial thromboses due to increased coagulability. Polycythemia is often associated with pruritus, visual disturbances, erythromelalgia, coronary and cerebral thromboses, and Budd–Chiari syndrome (hepatic vein occlusion). Pulmonary hypertension occurs with increased frequency in this population.<sup>266</sup> Polycythemia vera is diagnosed in patients with elevated hematocrit levels (greater than 49 in men and 48 in women) or Hb concentrations (greater than 16.5 g/dL in men, or 16 g/dL in women), trilineage hypercellularity, and a _JAK2_ mutation.<sup>266</sup>

### Preoperative

Patients with polycythemia vera are at increased risk of both thrombosis and hemorrhage. Younger patients with no history of thrombosis can be treated with regular phlebotomies to maintain hematocrits less than 45%, in addition to aspirin. Patients with more severe disease benefit from myelosuppression with hydroxyurea, busulfan, or interferon. The JAK inhibitor ruxolitinib may be necessary in refractory cases.

Patients with platelet counts above 1 million are prone to thrombotic and hemorrhagic complications. The hemorrhagic tendency is due to an acquired von Willebrand syndrome likely due to von Willebrand factor (vWF) binding to thrombocytes and their destruction due to hyperviscosity. Hydroxyurea decreases platelet counts and is an effective treatment for the acquired von Willebrand syndrome. Patients should continue their outpatient polycythemia medications, and have a hematocrit less than 45%. The decision to continue or hold aspirin prior to surgery is determined by weighing the relative bleeding versus thrombotic risk.

### Intraoperative

These patients are at risk of both thrombosis and hemorrhage. Maintaining hydration intraoperatively helps to maintain a relatively normal hematocrit and prevent thrombo- hemorrhagic complications. The acquired von Willebrand syndrome can be treated with factor VIII and vWF concentrates or desmopressin. Avoidance of neuraxial anesthesia is best.

### Postoperative

Patients are monitored for thrombo- hemorrhagic complications with co- management with hematologists.

### ESSENTIAL THROMBOCYTHEMIA

Essential thrombocythemia is a myeloproliferative disorder associated with J _AK2_ , _CALR_ , or _MPL_ mutations in 90% of cases. The diagnosis is based on persistent platelet counts greater than 450,000, megakaryocyte proliferation in the



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 52 頁）

![第 29 章，原始 PDF 第 52 頁](assets/chapter-29/source-page-0052.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 53 -->

bone marrow, and one of the gene mutations. Patients can be asymptomatic, or have vasomotor symptoms (lightheadedness, livedo reticularis), or thrombotic or hemorrhagic events. Predictors of arterial thrombosis are age older than 60 years, history of thrombosis, cardiovascular risk factors (e.g., smoking, hypertension, diabetes mellitus), white blood cell count greater than 11,000/mcL, and the _JAK2_ V617F mutation. Thrombotic events include stroke, transient ischemic attacks, retinal artery and venous occlusions, coronary artery ischemia, pulmonary embolism, hepatic and portal vein thrombosis, deep vein thrombosis, and Raynaud disease. Hemorrhagic complications are due to an acquired von Willebrand syndrome similar to that described for polycythemia vera.<sup>266</sup>

### Preoperative

It is unclear what is a safe platelet count for surgery. The decision to hold aspirin perioperatively is made by weighing the relative risks of bleeding versus thrombosis. Cytoreductive agents should be continued. In patients with a prior history of thrombosis, anticoagulants are managed as per current guidelines.<sup>268</sup>

### Intraoperative

Patients are monitored for thrombo- hemorrhagic complications with co- management with hematologists.

### Postoperative

Postoperative considerations pertain to the thrombohemorrhagic tendencies, as discussed.

### VON WILLEBRAND DISEASE

Von Willebrand disease (vWD) is the most commonly encountered platelet- mediated bleeding disorder. It is inherited as either an autosomal dominant or autosomal recessive trait. Patients with vWD usually present with mucocutaneous bleeding (epistaxis, menorrhagia, bruising, or gingival). Diagnosis should be prompted by a history of hemorrhage, as vWF concentrations can be low in patients with no clinically relevant bleeding, leading to an overdiagnosis. Patients with a history of mucocutaneous bleeding, with a positive family history or a prolonged aPTT in the presence of mild thrombocytopenia should be screened with routine coagulation tests, vWF antigen and activity, in addition to factor VIII concentrations. Patients with a positive family or personal diagnosis of bleeding are diagnosed with vWD if their vWF (antigen or activity) is less than 30% and “low” vWD if vWF (antigen or activity) is 30%–50%.<sup>266</sup> Furthermore, all patients diagnosed with vWD should have vWF multimer analysis testing to establish the specific type of vWD, as management differs. The majority of patients with vWD have type 1 (80%). These patients have recurrent episodes of severe bleeding due to significantly decreased vWF concentrations (less than 15%–25% of normal). Type 2 vWD is due to reduction of large vWF multimers, resulting in a significant decrease in vWF activity. Patients with type 3 vWD present with a hemophilia- type syndrome (hemarthroses, muscle bleeding) that results from a lack of vWF antigen and severely decreased concentrations of vWF activity and factor VIII (3%–10% of normal).

### Preoperative

Preoperative preparation relies on obtaining a detailed bleeding history, accurate diagnosis, and prior treatment interventions. Generally, patients with vWF levels >40% do not require any further perioperative interventions, as such levels confer virtually normal hemostasis.

### Intraoperative

Intraoperative management depends on the type of vWD and the invasiveness of the planned procedure. Options include desmopressin and vWF concentrates. Desmopressin releases vWF from the endothelial cells and benefits patients with type 1 disease and some patients with type 2 vWD having minor procedures. There is a complete lack of response to desmopressin patients with type 3 disease.<sup>269,270</sup> Desmopressin is administered intravenously at a dose of 0.3 mcg/kg 30 minutes before procedures. In general, a trial of desmopressin is administered preoperatively to assess responsiveness. Those with very mild type 1 vWD (activity greater than 30%) do not require a trial as they are routinely responsive. If desmopressin is administered it may need to be redosed intra- and postoperatively when major bleeding is expected. Repeated doses of desmopressin may cause hyponatremia, so sodium concentrations should be monitored. Desmopressin can reduce bleeding and transfusion requirements during major procedures, but vWF concentrates may be needed as well. vWF concentrates are indicated for patients with type 3 vWF disease undergoing any procedures. Some preparations (Humate- P, Alphanate, and Wilate) contain factor VIII. Others (Vonvendi) do not, and factor VIII may need to be supplemented. Antifibrinolytics can be used as adjunctive therapy during surgical procedures and platelet transfusion may benefit patients with platelet counts less than 50,000.

### Postoperative

Bleeding risk remains elevated postoperatively. vWF concentrates may need to be continued until bleeding risk decreases.

### HEMOPHILIA A

Hemophilia A is due to deletions or mutations to the X chromosome, manifesting only in males and resulting in very low concentrations of factor VIII. Patients with factor VIII activity below 1% of normal have frequent spontaneous bleeding into joints, muscle, or other organs. Those with factor VIII concentrations above 1% to 30% may not bleed spontaneously but be at risk of bleeding with surgery.<sup>271</sup> The diagnosis is suspected when there is a prolonged aPTT with a normal PT and is confirmed with measuring factor VIII levels. Treatment includes prophylactic factor VIII concentrates in patients with spontaneous bleeding, or emicizumab. Emicizumab is a recombinant monoclonal antibody that binds to factors IXa and X simultaneously, obviating the need for factor VIIIa as a cofactor to activate factor X.

### Preoperative

The severity of the hemophilia, treatments, presence of factor inhibitors, response to desmopressin (typically only those with mild disease), and the bleeding risk of

<!-- PDF page 54 -->

the procedure need to be determined. Patients with mild hemophilia A having minor procedures benefit from desmopressin, which can sufficiently increase factor VIII levels. Patients with hemophilia A who receive factor VIII concentrates may develop inhibitor antibodies, and subsequently do not respond to factor VIII concentrates. The inhibitor activity is detected and quantified preoperatively.

### Intraoperative

For minor procedures in patients with mild hemophilia, desmopressin administered 30 minutes before incision may be sufficient. For major procedures, factor VIII concentrations must be increased to 80%–100%. For minor procedures, factor VIII concentrations are increased to 50%–80%.<sup>271</sup> Factor VIII concentrate must be administered 30–60 minutes preoperatively and repeated every 8–12 hours. Factor VIII concentrate has a half- life of 12 hours. Fresh frozen plasma and cryoprecipitate can also correct factor VIII deficiency. Antifibrinolytics can be given as adjuncts for superficial surgeries.

Patients with inhibitor antibodies to factor VIII require special treatment. Those with low antibody titers may still benefit from factor VIII concentrates, whereas patients with high titers require activated prothrombin complex concentrate, a factor VIII inhibitor bypassing agent (FEIBA), or recombinant factor VII.<sup>270</sup> Patients taking emicizumab who also have inhibitor antibodies should be treated with a FEIBA only while monitored for thrombotic events and thrombotic microangiopathy.

### Postoperative

The treatments described above should be continued for a few weeks postoperatively to prevent bleeding complications.

### HEMOPHILIA B

Patients with hemophilia B (characterized by a deficiency of factor IX) have a clinical spectrum of disease similar to that found in hemophilia A. Patients with factor IX levels less than 1% of normal can have severe bleeding episodes, whereas those with factor IX concentrations between 5% and 40% have mild disease.<sup>272</sup> Laboratory findings are similar to hemophilia A, with a prolonged aPTT and a normal PT.

### Preoperative

Evaluation and management (with the exception of desmopressin) of patients with hemophilia B are similar to those for management of hemophilia A patients. The patient’s current therapy, a detailed bleeding history, factor IX concentrations, and presence of inhibitors should be ascertained.

### Intraoperative

Desmopressin is not an option for patients with hemophilia B. Recombinant or purified factor IX product or factor IX–prothrombin complex concentrate is used to treat mild bleeding episodes or is administered prophylactically for minor surgery. For major procedures (especially orthopedic, liver, trauma), thromboses are possible with factor IX–prothrombin complex concentrates as they contain activated clotting factors. Therefore, only

purified factor IX or recombinant factor IX should be used in these circumstances. Hemophilia B patients with factor IX inhibitors can be managed in acute situations using recombinant factor VIIa (preferred) or a prothrombin complex concentrate.

### Postoperative

Similar to hemophilia A, factor IX concentrates or recombinant products should be administered for several days postoperatively in patients with hemophilia B.

### THROMBOCYTOPENIA

Thrombocytopenia is defined as a platelet count of less than 150,000. The etiology of thrombocytopenia is varied and presented in Table 29.31.

### Preoperative

Platelet counts are optimized depending on the severity of thrombocytopenia, the etiology, and the invasiveness of the procedure. The need for red cell transfusion is increased at all levels of thrombocytopenia and increases with lower platelet counts. Targeted platelet counts differ for various procedures<sup>273</sup> (Table 29.32). Platelet counts above 50,000 are sufficient for most surgeries. However, platelet counts above 100,000 are recommended for spinal and intracranial procedures and neuraxial anesthesia. Please refer to Chapter 58 for recommendations for platelet count targets for obstetric patients.

Specific medications or therapies are recommended for different thrombocytopenia types. Some patients will require platelet transfusions. Monoclonal antibodies such as eculizumab may be of use in patients at high risk of alloimmunization due to repeated transfusions.<sup>274</sup> Patients with idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP) benefit from steroids or intravenous immunoglobulin infusions timed so that the peak response coincides with the surgical procedure. In addition, patients with ITP benefit from thrombopoietin receptor agonists (e.g., romiplostim, avatrombopag, and eltrombopag) leading to an increase in platelet counts by as much as 50,000 12–14 days after therapy.<sup>275,276</sup> Similarly, thrombopoietin receptor agonists (e.g., avatrombopag, eltrombopag, and lusutrombopag) result in a similar platelet count increase 5–8 days after a treatment course.<sup>277</sup>

Patients with thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP) and hemolytic uremic syndrome benefit from plasmapheresis. Patients with drug- induced thrombocytopenia or disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) may respond to treatment of the underlying cause (e.g., sepsis, drug withdrawal).

### Intraoperative

Intraoperative management is focused on prevention of bleeding, by maintaining platelet counts as well as judicious use of adjuncts. In addition to platelet transfusions, adjuncts such as antifibrinolytics have been successfully used in cardiac, orthopedic, and trauma surgeries. Platelet transfusions should be used with caution in patients with consumptive processes such as DIC, TTP, or heparininduced thrombocytopenia as thrombotic complications can result. But platelets should not be withheld in bleeding patients.<sup>278–280</sup>
